Term Assignment and Categorical Models for Intuitionistic Linear Logic with Subexponentials The author is sincerely grateful to Grigory Kondyrev for providing quite a few insightful and original ideas and for inspiring the author to revise his approach in many aspects. The author is also thankful to Anton Ayzenberg, Murdoch James Gabbay, Andrei Krutikov and Nikita Repeev for remarks improving the ideas of the paper. The author also thanks Stepan Kuznetsov and Andre Scedrov for the initial impulse resulted in this research.
Daniel Rogozin

Abstract
In this paper, we present a typed lambda calculus 𝐒𝐈𝐋𝐋 ​ ( λ ) Σ {\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma} , a type-theoretic version of intuitionistic linear logic with subexponentials, that is, we have many resource comonadic modalities with some interconnections between them given by a subexponential signature. We also give proof normalisation rules and prove the strong normalisation and Church-Rosser properties for β \beta -reduction by adapting the Tait-Girard method to subexponential modalities. Further, we analyse subexponentials from the point of view of categorical logic. We introduce the concepts of a Cocteau category and a Σ \Sigma -assemblage to characterise models of linear type theories with a single exponential and affine and relevant subexponentials and a more general case respectively. We also generalise several known results from linear logic and show that every Cocteau category and a Σ \Sigma -assemblage can be viewed as a symmetric monoidal closed category equipped with a family of monoidal adjunctions of a particular kind. In the final section, we give a stronger 2-categorical characterisation of Cocteau categories.



Keywords—
Linear logic, symmetric monoidal categories, subexponentials, comonad, Eilenberg-Moore categories, relevant categories, monoidal adjunction

1
Introduction

The exponential modality
\bang
is a comonadic operator allowing one to introduce the lacking weakening and contraction
inference rules in linear logic
[
Gir87
]
.
\bang
is given by the so-called promotion and dereliction rules
(aka introduction and elimination) rules alongside the weakening and contraction rules for modalised formulas.
The standard presentation consists of the below Gentzen-style inference rules:

\bang A_{1},\ldots,\bang A_{n}\vdash A

\bang_{\bf L}

\bang A_{1},\ldots,\bang A_{n}\vdash\bang A

\Gamma\vdash B

{\bf W}

\Gamma,\bang A\vdash B

\Gamma,A\vdash B

\bang_{\bf R}

\Gamma,\bang A\vdash B

\Gamma,\bang A,\bang A\vdash B

{\bf C}

\Gamma,\bang A\vdash B

Subexponentials are operators of the form
\bang_{s}
that may allow (or not) weakening or contraction where
s
ranges over some finite set of indices.
We have a family of non-equivalent modal operators
\bang_{s}
indexed through elements of a finite set
I
, and each
\bang_{s}
has its own ability of introducing
this or that structural rule. Subexponential modalities were initially introduced in
[
DJS93
]

as part of “multicolour linear logic” and then developed in
[
NOP17
]

to propose a proof-theoretic framework for concurrency theory and linear logic programming.
We refer the reader interested in practial uses of subexponential to
[
NM09
]
, where the authors
claim to develop a simple specification language expressive enough to model data structures and iterative procedures.
Subexponentials (and, more generally, weaker versions of the exponential modality) in a non-commutative setting are also of interest in mathematical linguistics
as it has been discussed in
[
KKNS19
]
and
[
MSWW23
]
.
From a linguistic point of view, structural rules are generally pathologic since they change the structure of a sentence,
so they might not stay grammatically correct. Still, the contraction rule allows dealing with such cases, where the localised
duplication is unavoidable, for example, in analysing so-called ’parasitic extractions’, as it was elaborated in
[
KKS17
]
.

Linear logic also provides a framework for functional programming with more flexible mechanisms of resource management
by adapting ideas of the Curry-Howard correspondence, see
[
Wad90
]
and
[
Gir87
]
.
Many of those ideas have already found their practical manifestation (to a certian extent) in such functional languages
as Haskell (
[
BBN+17
]
) and Idris 2 (
[
Bra21
]
). One can transfer the same ideas
to other substructural logics, in particular affine logic admitting weakening and relevant logic admitting contractions.
Programs well-typed in affine type lambda calculus, for example, are known to terminate in linear time (see
[
Cur05
, §1]
),
whereas type systems based on linear logic are of interest in term of compiler optimisations, see
[
Wal05
]
.

As regards the questions of semantics, linear logic has a number of semantic frameworks reflecting the intuition
that we interpret provability through resource-aware actions, not statements being valid in the sense of classical or intuitionistic logic,
see
[
Gir11
, Part III, Part VI]
for a more detailed discussion on geometry of interaction and coherence spaces.
Categorical semantics of linear logic, as it is presented, for example, in
[
AT11
]
and
[
Mel09
]
,
generalises the formal properties of coherence spaces and allows interpreting proofs as morphisms in symmetric monoidal categories:
this gives an account of linear logic as the so-called
internal logic
of symmetric monoidal categories in the fashion
of category-theoretic approach to metamathematics
[
LS88
]
.

In this paper, we introduce a type-theoretic formulation of multiplicative intuitionistic linear logic
with subexponentials in several versions. We consider a linear type theory called
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{3}
with the three subexponential modal operators

\bang_{\bf i}
,
\bang_{\bf r}
and
\bang_{\bf a}
having the following informal semantics:

•

M:\bang_{\bf i}A
means that
M
is an element of type
A
that we can use finitely many times.
So
\bang_{\bf i}
has the behaviour of the usual
\bang
operator from linear logic.
That is,
M
is an element of an
intuitionistic
(or
normal
) type.

•

M:\bang_{\bf r}A
means that
M
is an element of
A
that one should consume
at least
once.
In other words,
M
is an element of a
relevant
type.

•

M:\bang_{\bf a}A
means that
M
is an element of
A
that one should consume
at most
once.
In other words,
M
is an element of an
affine
type.

{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{3}
also contains the following principle introducing a naturally occurring policy
of how several modes of resource management are connected with each other. Informally, this principle can be formulated
with two (meta)implications (aka symmetric lax monoidal comonad morphisms in category theoretic terms):
\bang_{\bf i}A\Rightarrow\bang_{\bf r}A
and
\bang_{\bf i}A\Rightarrow\bang_{\bf a}A
.
That is, anything that one can duplicate (destroy) an object in a “normal” way,
then it can be duplicated (or destroyed) as an object of a relevant (or affine) type. However, this is not the only way of expanding linear logic with modalities since
in many cases the presence of many non-equivalent subexponentials with more complicated interaction between them
is also of interest, for example, in concurrent programming, see
[
OPN15
]
. So we develop
the Curry-Howard correspondence for a bigger family of subexponential enrichments of intuitionistic linear logic
alongside their categorical semantics.

In categorical semantics of linear logic, the exponential modality corresponds to a comonad associating
a cocommutative comonoid with every object, see
[
AT11
, §1.8.4]
,
[
See87
]
,
[
Tro92
, Chapter 12]
and
[
BBDPH93a
]
.. In terms of the computational interpretation,
\bang
allows
easing the resource usage restrictions: by default any thing can be used exactly ones, but with
\bang

we can locally address to a particular formula as many times as we want. Logically,
\bang
allows embedding
intuitionistic logic into linear logic (and there is a similar result for classical logic as well)
and manipulating with duplications and deletions in a restricted way being consistent with the ideology of linear logic.

Although subexponentials have been already studied quite comprehensively from the aspects of proof theory, linguistics
and such topics in applied computer science as epistemic and spatial concurrent constraint programming (see
[
NOP17
, §7-8]
),
the semantic aspects of subexponentials have remained overlooked. Although such aspects do not play a significant role
in developing practical frameworks, the semantic analysis would allow us to equip with subexponentials with a rigorous meaning
and also provide the framework with several resource policies within a single system with the denotational semantics.

1.1
Map of the paper

In Section
2
, we axiomatise a family of natural deduction calculi with proof-terms
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}

with proof normalisation rules. We favoured the Curry-Howard style presentation (see
[
SU06
]
to have a more general perspective)
of all those calculi for two reasons. First, given such a presentation, one can think of
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}

(in particular, its instance for a three-element subexponential signature) as a type-theoretic foundation for a strongly linearly typed functional programming language with several policies
of how the user consumes data. Secondly, proof-terms in our approach come along with the corresponing proof-conversion rules,
so if proof-terms reflect morphisms in syntax, then the conversion rules syntactically represent the axioms of a Cocteau category.
Thus, the semantic interpretation is defined for both derivability and proof transformations.
We also prove the strong normalisation and confluence properties for
\beta
-reduction by developing a standard technique in the fashion of Tait-Girard.

The rest of the sections in the paper study to give a semantic framework for
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
and
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{3}

and how categorical models of those systems can be represented.
In Section
4
, we introduce the notions of a Cocteau category, that is, a symmetric monoidal closed
category with three lax monoidal comonads: exponential, affine and relevant.
We also introduce the concept of a
\Sigma
-assemblage which generalises Cocteau categories directly for an arbitrary
subexponential signature
\Sigma
. We show that every
\Sigma
-assemblage is a model of
the multisorted equational theory generated by
{\bf SILL}_{\Sigma}(\lambda)
-proof conversions.

In Section
5
, we elaborate on how one can characterise so-called relevant categories,
that is, symmetric monoidal categories where each object
A
can be copied with a natural transformation
\gamma

with the components
\gamma_{A}:A\to A\otimes A
. We treat relevant categories separately because of the important difference
between them and Cartesian categories in the following aspect. It is well known that a symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}

is Cartesian if and only the forgetful functor
{\bf coMon}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
is an isomorphism, see, e.g.,
[
Mel09
, Corollary 19]
,
where
{\bf coMon}(\mathcal{C})
is the category of cocommutative comonoids in
\mathcal{C}
.
As it has been already discussed in
[
rel
]
, we only have a weaker criterion: a symmetric monoidal category
is relevant if and only if the forgetful functor
{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
has a strict symmetric monoidal section. The aforementioned
nLab article gives neither a complete proof of this fact nor a reference, so we prove this fact in Section
5

as it seems it has not been introduced in such a form earlier in the literature.

In Section
6
, we modify Benton’s results from
[
Ben94
]
connecting
linear-non-linear models and linear categories. We introduce Marais models and
\Sigma
-boutons
to characterise Cocteau categories and
\Sigma
-assemblages with symmetric monoidal categories
equipped with resource modalities using the characterisation results on relevant categories from the previous section.
Generally, we unveil each comonad as a monoidal adjunction with the corresponding Eilenberg-Moore categories of coalgebras.

In Section
7
, we provide the 2-categorical approach based on the formal theory of comonads
to characterising the category of Cocteau categories with monoidal adjunctions,
but we also treat functors of Cocteau categories and their natural transformations.

2
System
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)
and its syntactic properties

We first recall the underlying logic called
{\bf SILL}_{\Sigma}
and then develop a subexponential
linear type theory by assigning linear lambda terms to the inference rules of
{\bf SILL}_{\Sigma}
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
(I,\preceq)
be a finite preorder, a
subexponential signature

is a quadruple
\Sigma=(I,\preceq,W,C)
where
W
and
C

are upward closed subsets of
I
.

The intuitive idea standing behind the notion of a subexponential signature is that
we have a preordered finite family of indices of subexponential modal operators
\{\bang_{s}\>|\>s\in I\}

and
W
and
C
are subsets of those indices whose operators admit weakening and contraction respectively.
The preorder relation on subexponential indices has the following informal semantics. If
s_{1}\preceq s_{2}
,
the operator
\bang_{1}
is stronger than
\bang_{2}
, that is,
\bang_{s_{2}}A\Rightarrow\bang_{s_{1}}A
.
This is why we require
W
and
C
to be increasing: the property of admitting weakening or contraction
is inherited by stronger modalities from weaker ones. We capture this idea rigorously further.

The definition of a well-formed formula is specified by the corresponding grammar.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\Sigma=(I,\preceq,W,C)
be a subexponential signature and let
\{p_{i}\>|\>i<\omega\}

be a countable set of propositional variables, the set of
\Sigma
-formulas is generated by the following grammar:

A,B::=p_{i}\>|\>\mathds{1}\>|\>(A\multimap B)\>|\>(A\otimes B)\>|\>(\bang_{s}A)_{s\in\Sigma}

By default, subexponentials bind stronger than binary connectives, whereas
the tensor product connective has a higher priority than linear implication.

If
s\in W(C)
, then
\bang_{s}
is an
affine
(
relevant
) subexponential.
\bang_{s}
is
exponential

if
s\in W\cap C
.

A
context
is a finite sequence of formulas. Capital Greek letters
\Gamma,\Delta,\Theta
denote arbitrary contexts.

\Gamma,A
and
\Gamma,\Delta
standardly mean
\Gamma\cup\{A\}
and
\Gamma\cup\Delta
.
A sequent is a pair
\Gamma\Rightarrow A
that we read as “
A
is derivable from
\Gamma
”.

The natural deduction calculus with introduce is piggybacked on
[
BBDPH93b
]
,
but we expand it with the additional inference rules for affine and relevant subexponentials.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\Sigma=(I,\preceq,W,C)
be a subexponential signature.
The calculus
{\bf SILL}_{\Sigma}
is axiomatised with the axioms and inference rules given in Figure
1
.

Figure 1:
Natural deduction calculus
{\bf SILL}_{\Sigma}

\mathds{1}{\bf I}

\vdash\mathds{1}

ax

A\vdash A

\Gamma\vdash\mathds{1}

\Delta\vdash B

\mathds{1}{\bf E}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash B

\Gamma,A\vdash B

\multimap{\bf I}

\Gamma\vdash A\multimap B

\Gamma\vdash A

\Delta\vdash B

\otimes{\bf I}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash A\otimes B

\Gamma\vdash A\multimap B

\Delta\vdash A

\multimap{\bf E}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash B

\Gamma\vdash A\otimes B

\Delta,A,B\vdash C

\otimes{\bf E}

\Delta,\Gamma\vdash C

\Gamma_{1}\vdash\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n}\vdash\bang_{s_{n}}A_{n}

\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\ldots,\bang_{s_{n}}A_{n}\vdash A

{\bang_{s}}{\bf I}

\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n}\vdash\bang_{s}A

for
s\preceq s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}

\Gamma\vdash\bang_{s}A

{\bang_{s}}{\bf E}

\Gamma\vdash A

for
s\in\Sigma

\Gamma\vdash\bang_{s}A

\Delta\vdash B

{\bf W}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash B

for
s\in W

\Gamma\vdash\bang_{s}A

\Delta,\bang_{s}A,\bang_{s}A\vdash B

{\bf C}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash B

for
s\in C

One can also formulate the sequent-style formulation of
{\bf SILL}_{\Sigma}
and prove the cut elimination and the subformula
properties similarly to
[
KKNS19
, Theorem 2]
and
[
NOP17
, Theorem 1]
.

2.1
Assigning Proof Terms

The formalism we present below is inspired by
[
Tro95
]
and
[
BBDPH93a
]
. In fact, we can think of it as the expansion of the
linear type theory with additional relevent and affine subexponentials
allowing the structural weakening and contraction rules respectively. We obtain the typing rules of the type theory
by labelling
{\bf SILL}_{\Sigma}
with terms as in Figure
2
.

Let us define linear terms first.

Definition 2.4

(Preterms)
.

Let
\Sigma=(I,\preceq,W,C)
and let
\operatorname{Var}=\{x_{i}\>|\>i<\omega\}
be a countable alphabet of variables.
The set of preterms is generated by the following BNF-grammar.

\begin{array}[]{lll}&M,N::=x_{i}\>|\>{\bf 1}\>|\>{\bf let}\>{\bf 1}=M\>{\bf in}\>N\>|\>\lambda x:A.M\>|\>MN\>|\>M\otimes N\>|\>{\bf let}\>x\otimes y\>=\>M\>{\bf in}\>N\>|&\\
&\>\>\>\>(\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{N})_{s\in\Sigma}\>|\>({\bf der}_{s}(M))_{s\in\Sigma}\>|\>({\bf del}_{s}(M;N))_{s\in W}\>|\>({\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=M\>{\bf in}\>N)_{s\in W}&\end{array}

A preterm
M
is a
linear lambda term
(or simply
term
) if it is well-typed, that is, there is a context
\Gamma
and a type
A
such that
\Gamma\vdash M:A

is derivable by the typing rules from Figure
2
.

Let us introduce the following shorthand notation for a tuple of terms
\vec{M}=(M_{0},\ldots,M_{n})
:

•

{\bf del}_{s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}}(\vec{M};N)\leftrightharpoons{\bf del}_{s_{0}}(M_{0};({\bf del}_{s_{1}}(M_{1};\ldots({\bf del}_{s_{n}}(M_{n};N))\ldots)))
,

•

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{y},\vec{z}=\vec{M}\>{\bf in}\>N\leftrightharpoons{\bf let}_{s}\>y_{0},z_{0}=M_{0}\>{\bf in}\>({\bf let}_{s}\>y_{1},z_{1}=M_{1}\>{\bf in}\>(\ldots({\bf let}_{s}\>y_{n},z_{n}=M_{n}\>{\bf in}\>N)\ldots))
.

So we assume that every free variable occurs exactly once as in the term assignment for a multiplicative-exponential fragment intuitionistic linear logic.

A
context

\Gamma
is a finite multiset of declarations of the form
x:A
, where
x
is a variable and

A
is a formula. A typing judgement is a typing declaration
M:A
for a term
M
.

\Gamma\vdash M:A
stands for “a typing judgement
M:A
is provable from
\Gamma
”. The type inference rules are given in Figure
2
.

Such a term assignment directly generalises the linear typed lambda calculus for intuitionistic linear logic from,
for example,
[
Tro92
, Chapter 6]
for an arbitrary subexponential signature
\Sigma
.

Figure 2:
System
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}

\mathds{1}{\bf I}

\vdash{\bf 1}:\mathds{1}

ax

x:A\vdash x:A

\Gamma\vdash M:\mathds{1}

\Delta\vdash N:B

\mathds{1}{\bf E}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash{\bf let}\>{\bf 1}=M\>{\bf in}\>N:B

\Gamma,x:A\vdash M:B

\multimap{\bf I}

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M:A\multimap B

\Gamma\vdash M:A

\Delta\vdash N:B

\otimes{\bf I}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M\otimes N:A\otimes B

\Gamma\vdash M:A\multimap B

\Delta\vdash N:A

\multimap{\bf E}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash MN:B

\Gamma\vdash M:A\otimes B

\Delta,x:A,y:B\vdash N:C

\otimes{\bf E}

\Delta,\Gamma\vdash{\bf let}\>x\otimes y\>=\>M\>{\bf in}\>N:C

\Gamma_{1}\vdash M_{1}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n}\vdash M_{n}:\bang_{s_{n}}A_{n}

x_{1}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:\bang_{s_{n}}A_{n}\vdash N:A

{\bang_{s}}

\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n}\vdash\bang_{s}\>N\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}:\bang_{s}A

where
s\preceq s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
.

\Gamma\vdash M:\bang_{s}A

{\bang_{s}}_{E}

\Gamma\vdash{\bf der}_{s}\>M:A

for each
s\in I
.

\Gamma\vdash M:\bang_{s}A

\Delta\vdash N:B

{\bf W}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash{\bf del}_{s}(M;N):B

for
s\in W
.

\Gamma\vdash M:\bang_{s}A

\Delta,x:\bang_{s}A,y:\bang_{s}A\vdash N:B

{\bf C}

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash{\bf let}_{s}x,y=M\>{\bf in}\>N:B

for
s\in C
.

We do not trouble ourselves with the rigorous definitions of free variables and linear substitution,
the reader can transfer them from
[
Bie94
, Definition 3.14, Definition 3.15]
.

Lemma 2.5

(Substitution Lemma)
.

If

\Gamma\vdash M:A
and
\Delta,x:A\vdash N:B
, then
\Delta,\Gamma\vdash N[x:=M]:B
.

Proof.

Induction on the derivation of
\Delta,x:A\vdash N:B
.
∎

Let
\Gamma
be a context of free variable declarations
\{x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}\}
, then
let
|\Gamma|=\{A_{i}\>|\>i=1,\ldots,n,x_{i}:A_{i}\in\Gamma\}
.

There is a particular subexponential signature that we shall consider separately.
Let
I
be a three-element set
\{{\bf i},{\bf r},{\bf a}\}
. The mnemonic is that

{\bf i}
,
{\bf r}
and
{\bf a}
stand for “intuitionistic”, “relevant” and “affine” respectively.
The preorder relation is given by the below graph:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\preceq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\succeq}

This preorder depicts that idea if we have an object that we can use finitely many times,
such an object can be duplicated (and, thus, become relevant) and destroyed (and, thus, become affine). The subsets

W
and
C
are
\{{\bf a},{\bf i}\}
and
\{{\bf r},{\bf i}\}
respectively.
By
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{3}
, we denote
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
over the above described signature three element signature.

Although
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{3}
is obtained from
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
, we would like to consider them
as different calculi.
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{3}
describes the natural way of how different policies of resource
management interact with one another, whereas
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
describe a broader class
of polymodal intuitionistic linear logics where some of the subexponentials might have nothing to do resource management at all.

2.2
Proof Normalisation

As for linear type theory with the single exponential modality
\bang
considered in
[
BBDPH93b
]
, one can distinguish the
\beta\eta
-reduction relation
and the commuting conversion relation which are conversions between terms occuring from the proof normalisation analysis.
Although all those commuting conversions can be transfered to
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)
, we consider a simpler approach
that combines
[
Tro95
]
and
[
Bie94
, §4.3]
.
First of all we specify the term conversions for
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
.

•

The
\beta
-conversions specified in Figure
3
describe so-called
detour conversions

1
1

1

Troesltra and Schwichtenberg use the term
detour contractions
, but we would reserve the word “contraction”
for the corresponding structural rule.
. Those conversions are rather common in many presentation of proof contractions for
intuitionistic linear logic.

Figure 3:
\beta
-conversions

(\multimap\beta)

(\lambda x.M)N\triangleleft M[x:=N]
,

(\mathds{1}\beta)

{\bf let}\>{\bf 1}={\bf 1}\>{\bf in}\>N\triangleleft N
,

(\otimes\beta)

{\bf let}\>x\otimes y=M\otimes N\>{\bf in}\>P\triangleleft P[x:=M,y:=N]
,

(\bang_{s}\beta)

{\bf der}_{s}\>(\bang_{s}\>N\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M})\triangleleft N[\vec{x}:=\vec{M}]
for
s\in\Sigma
,

({\bf W}\beta)

Let
s\preceq s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
and
s\in W
, then

{\bf del}_{s}({\bang_{s}}N\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M},N^{\prime})\triangleleft{\bf del}_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}}(\vec{M},N^{\prime})

({\bf C}\beta)

Let
s\in C

{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=(\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M^{\prime}})\>{\bf in}\>N\triangleleft{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{x^{\prime}},\vec{x^{\prime\prime}}=\vec{M^{\prime}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{x^{\prime}},z:=\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{x^{\prime\prime}}].

•

The extensional
\eta
-conversions from Figure
4
can be considered as
simplification conversions
in the derivations
where an introduction rule was used after applying the corresponding elimination rule.
In type-theoretical terms, simplification conversions correspond to
\eta
-reduction.

Figure 4:
\eta
-conversions

(\multimap\eta)

(\lambda x.Mx)\triangleleft M
,

(\bang_{s}\eta)

\bang_{s}({\bf der}_{s}\>x)\>{\bf with}\>x=M\triangleleft M
,

(\otimes\eta)

{\bf let}\>x\otimes y=M\>{\bf in}\>N[z:=x\otimes y]\triangleleft N[z:=M]
.

•

The conversions from Figure
5
syntactically depict that the tensor product and identity types
alongside the weakening and contraction operations
satisfy the categorical naturality property. From a proof-theoretic angle,
those conversions reflect
permutation conversions
(again, the terminology is due to Troelstra) where we permute minor premises of an elimination rule in a certian way.

Figure 5:
The naturality conversions for
\mathds{1}
,
\otimes
, contraction and weakening

(\otimes_{\bf nat})

P[w:={\bf let}\>x\otimes y=M\>{\bf in}\>N]\triangleleft{\bf let}\>x\otimes y=M\>{\bf in}\>P[w:=N]
,

(\mathds{1}_{\bf nat})

P[w:={\bf let}\>{\bf 1}=M\>{\bf in}\>N]\triangleleft{\bf let}\>{\bf 1}=M\>{\bf in}\>P[w:=N]
,

({\bf del}_{s})_{\bf nat}

Let
s\in W
, then
P[x:={\bf del}_{s}(M;N)]\triangleleft{\bf del}_{s}(M;P[x:=N])
,

({\bf let}_{s})_{\bf nat}

Let
s\in C
, then
P[x:={\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=M\>{\bf in}\>N]\triangleleft{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=M\>{\bf in}\>P[x:=N]
.

•

The conversions from rest of the figures elaborate on how copying, deleting and promotion term constructors commute with one another.
In particular, the conversions having the form of
({\bf W}_{\operatorname{conv}})
say that the deletion operation has a coalgebraic nature,
but we can also view it as a proof conversion allowing us to avoid some redundant promotions when we combine
the promotion and deletion operations.

Categorically, the conversions
({\bf C}_{{\operatorname{conv}_{1}}})
,
({\bf C}_{{\operatorname{conv}_{2}}})
and
({\bf C}_{{\operatorname{conv}_{3}}})

demonstrate the coalgebraic and commutative comonoid nature of the contraction operation, but, as above,
we think of them as a separate sort of prooftree transformation that cannot be reduced to none of the above.

The conversion
({\bf CW}_{\operatorname{conv}})
specify how exactly
the exponential deletion and copying terms commute with each other. One can think of this conversion
as the syntactic encoding of one of the commutative comonoid axioms.

Figure 6:
The conversions for weakening and contraction

({\bf W}_{\operatorname{conv}})

Let
s,t\in\Sigma
,
s\in W
and
s\preceq t
, then

\bang_{t}({\bf del}_{s}(x;M))\>{\bf with}\>x,\vec{x}=N,\vec{N}\triangleleft{\bf del}_{t}(N;(\bang_{t}M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{N}))
,

({\bf C}_{{\operatorname{conv}_{1}}})

Let
s\preceq s_{1}
and
s\in C

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\bang_{s}\>({\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=x_{1}\>{\bf in}\>N)\>{\bf with}\>x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}=M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\triangleleft&\\
&\>\>\>\>{\bf let}_{s}\>y^{\prime},z^{\prime}=M_{1}\>{\bf in}\>(\bang_{s}N\>{\bf with}\>y^{\prime},z^{\prime},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}=y,z,M_{2},\ldots,M_{n})&\end{array}

({\bf C}_{{\operatorname{conv}_{2}}})

{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=M\>{\bf in}\>N\triangleleft{\bf let}_{s}\>y,x=M\>{\bf in}\>N
,

({\bf C}_{{\operatorname{conv}_{3}}})

{\bf let}_{s}\>x,w=\>M\>{\bf in}\>({\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=w\>{\bf in}\>N)\triangleleft{\bf let}_{s}\>w,z=M\>{\bf in}\>({\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=w\>{\bf in}\>N)
,

({\bf CW}_{\operatorname{conv}})

{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=M\>{\bf in}\>{\bf del}_{s}(x;N)\triangleleft N[y:=M]
for
s\in W\cap C
.

•

The conversion
({\bang_{s}}_{\bf conv})
was introduced by Troelstra for intuitionistic
linear logic, see
[
Tro95
, X1]
. We introduce the version of that conversion
for all subexponentials in a more general form.

Figure 7:
Extra
\bang_{s}
-conversion

(\bang_{t})_{\bf conv}

Assume that subexponential indices are agreed with one another in a reasonable way in terms of preorder in the
signature, then:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\bang_{t}M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{y^{\prime}},y,\vec{y^{\prime\prime}}=\vec{M^{\prime}},(\bang_{s}N\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}),\vec{M^{\prime\prime}}\triangleleft&\\
&\>\>\>\>\bang_{t}(M[y:=\bang_{s}N\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x^{\prime}}=\vec{x}])\>{\bf with}\>\vec{y^{\prime}},\vec{x^{\prime}},\vec{y^{\prime\prime}}=\vec{M^{\prime}},\vec{M},\vec{M^{\prime\prime}}&\end{array}

Definition 2.6
.

Let
M,N
be terms,
\Gamma
a context and
A
a type, a
conversion in context
is a judgement of the form

\Gamma\vdash M\triangleleft N:A
that we read as
M
converses to
N
in a context
\Gamma
and both
M
and
N

has type
A
. The conversion rules are specified in Figures
3
,
4
,
5
,
6

and
7
.
\trianglelefteq
(i.e. the
multistep conversion relation
) is the least preorder relation that contains
\triangleleft
.

An equality in context

\Gamma\vdash M\equiv N:A
is the symmetric closure of
\trianglelefteq
.

Theorem 2.7

(Normalisation)
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash M:A
be a typing judgement provable in
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
and
\Gamma\vdash M\trianglelefteq N:A
,
then the
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
-derivation of
\Gamma\vdash M:A
can be transformed into the
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
-derivation of

\Gamma\vdash N:A
.

Proof.

Induction on the generation of
M\triangleleft N
by using Lemma
2.5
.
∎

2.3
Strong Normalisation and Confluence for
\beta
-reduction

Now let focus on the conversion rules specified in Figure
3
and show a stronger fact than a mere subject reduction property for the

\beta
-reduction rule. As Benton showed in
[
Ben95
]
, one can embed
the linear term calculus into the second-order lambda calculus, also known as System F,
by using the technique of encoding coinductive data types with parametric polymorphism. It does not seem that
this idea is transferable indeed to subexponentials, both affine and relevant as well as those ones that do not
introduce any structural rules. So we develop the Girard method originally introduced
for System F in
[
Gir72
]
, but our presentation is generally based closer on
[
Bie94
, Theorem 18]
.
Although the proof is generally routine, we revisit it in order to check the subexponential-related clauses accurately and explicitly.

We provide the construction based on
\mathcal{P}
-candidates and computability types to deduce both the strong normalisation and confluence properties
to refine the presentation of
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
as a specification of a resource aware functional
programming language admitting several resource usage simultaneously. Although the strong normalisation is not
transferable to general-purpose languages for well-known reasons coming from the basics of computability theory, those facts still remain important
since they demonstrate why the evaluation process is determinate for programs built canonically from constructors and eliminators.
So the current goal is to prove the following:

Theorem 2.8
.

[Strong Normalisation for
\beta
-reduction]
Every
\beta
-reduction path
M_{0}\triangleleft_{\beta}M_{1}\triangleleft_{\beta}\ldots
terminates at a finite step.

We start by giving some preliminary definitions.

Definition 2.9
.

•

A
I
-term
is a result of applying the introduction rule, that is, a term of one of the following forms.

M\otimes N,\lambda x.N,\bang_{s}N\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}.

for arbitrary terms
M
,
N
and for an arbitrary sequence
\vec{M}
.

•

A term is called
simple
if it is not an
I
-term. A simple term
M
is
stubborn
if it is either has no redexes
amongst its subterms or
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}N
for some simple
N
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{{\bf P}_{A}\>|\>\text{$A$ is a type}\}
denote a family of non-empty sets of terms indexed with
the set of well-formed types. Let us define several properties of such families.

(P1)

x\in{\bf P}_{A}
for every variable of type
A
,

(P2)

M\triangleleft_{\beta}N
and
M\in{\bf P}_{A}
imply
N\in{\bf P}_{A}
.

(P3)

Let
M
be a simple term and

–

Assume
M\in{\bf P}_{A\multimap B}
,
N\in{\bf P}_{A}
and

(\lambda x.M^{\prime})N\in{\bf P}_{B}
for
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\lambda x.M^{\prime}
.
Then
MN\in{\bf P}_{B}
.

–

Let
s\in\Sigma
,
M\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s}A}
,

{\bf der}_{s}(\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M})\in{\bf P}_{A}
for

M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}
. Then
{\bf der}_{s}M\in{\bf P}_{A}
.

–

Let
s\in C
. Assume
M\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s}A}
,
N\in{\bf P}_{B}

and
{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=(\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M})\>{\bf in}\>N\in{\bf P}_{B}

for
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}
.
Then
{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=M\>{\bf in}\>N\in{\bf P}_{B}
.

–

Let
s\in W
. Assume
M\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s}A}
and
N\in{\bf P}_{B}
.
Assume that
{\bf del}_{s}(\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M};N)
for

M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}
,
then
{\bf del}_{s}(M;N)\in{\bf P}_{B}
.

(P4)

1.

If
M\in{\bf P}_{B}
and
x\in\operatorname{FV}(M)
, then
\lambda x.M\in{\bf P}_{A\multimap B}
,

2.

If
M\in{\bf P}_{A}
and
N\in{\bf P}_{B}
imply
M\otimes N\in{\bf P}_{A\otimes B}
,

3.

{\bf 1}\in{\bf P}_{\mathds{1}}
,

4.

Let
M\in{\bf P}_{A}
and
M_{1}\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}},\ldots,M_{n}\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s_{n}}A_{n}}

for
s\preceq s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
, then
\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}\in{\bf P}_{\bang sA}
.

(P5)

1.

Let
N\in{\bf P}_{A}
and
M[x:=N]\in{\bf P}_{B}
, then
(\lambda.M)N\in{\bf P}_{B}
,

2.

Let
M\in{\bf P}_{\mathds{1}}
and
N\in{\bf P}_{A}
, then
{\bf let}\>M={\bf 1}\>{\bf in}\>N\in{\bf P}_{A}
,

3.

Let
M\in{\bf P}_{A\otimes B}
and
N[x:=M^{\prime},y:=N^{\prime}]
for
x,y\in{\bf FV}(N)
and

M\triangleleft_{\beta}M^{\prime}\otimes N^{\prime}
,

4.

Let
s\in\Sigma
and
M\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s}A}

such that
M\triangleleft_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}
, then
{\bf der}_{s}M\in{\bf P}_{A}
.

5.

Let
s\in C
,
M\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s}A}
and
N\in{\bf P}_{B}
such that
{\bf P}_{B}
contains the following redex:

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M^{\prime}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]

for
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}
. Then
{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M\>{\bf in}\>N\in{\bf P}_{B}
.

6.

Let
M\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s}A}
,
N\in{\bf P}_{B}

and
{\bf del}_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}}(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n};N)\in{\bf P}_{B}

whenever
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}

and
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\succeq s
, then
{\bf del}_{s}(M;N)\in{\bf P}_{B}
.

A set
C_{A}
of terms of type
A
is called a
\mathcal{P}
-candidate
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(\mathcal{P}1)

C_{A}\subseteq{\bf P}_{A}
,

(\mathcal{P}2)

M\in C_{A}
and
M\triangleleft_{\beta}N
imply
N\in C_{A}
,

(\mathcal{P}3)

If
M\in{\bf P}_{A}
is simple and
M^{\prime}\in C_{A}
such that
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}
is an I-term,
then
M\in C
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a family of terms, let us associate the
computability type

{\bf ct}(A)

for each type
A
:

•

Let
p_{i}
be atomic, then
{\bf ct}(p_{i})={\bf P}_{A}
and
{\bf ct}(\mathds{1})={\bf P}_{\mathds{1}}
.

•

{\bf ct}(A\multimap B)=\{M\in{\bf P}_{A\multimap B}\>|\>\forall N\in{\bf ct}(A)\>MN\in{\bf ct}(B)\}
,

•

{\bf ct}(A\otimes B)=\{M\in{\bf P}_{A\otimes B}\>|\>M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}M_{1}\otimes M_{2}\Rightarrow M_{1}\in{\bf ct}(A)\>\&\>M_{2}\in{\bf ct}(B)\}
,

•

Let
s\in\Sigma
, then let
{\bf ct}^{\prime}(\bang_{s}A)
denote:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&{\bf ct}^{\prime}(\bang_{s}A)=\{M\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s}A}\>|\>{\bf der}_{s}(M)\in{\bf ct}(A)\>\&&\\
&\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>M_{1}\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}},\ldots,M_{n}\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s_{n}}A_{n}},s\preceq s_{1},\ldots,s_{n},M^{\prime}\in{\bf ct}(A)\>\text{for}\>M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}\}&\end{array}

Then if
s\in C
, then

\begin{array}[]{lll}&{\bf ct}(\bang_{s}A)={\bf ct}^{\prime}(\bang_{s}A)\cap\{M\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s}A}\>|\>M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}\>\&\>N\in{\bf P}_{A}\Rightarrow&\\
&\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>{\bf let}_{s}\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}{\bf with}\>\vec{u}=\vec{x},z:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}{\bf with}\>\vec{v}=\vec{x}]\}.&\end{array}

If
s\in W
, then

\begin{array}[]{lll}&{\bf ct}(\bang_{s}A)={\bf ct}^{\prime}(\bang_{s}A)\cap\{M\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s}A}\>|&\\
&\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}\>\&\>s\preceq s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\>\&\>N\in{\bf P}_{B}\Rightarrow{\bf del}_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}}(\vec{M};N)\in{\bf P}_{B}\}&\end{array}

otherwise we let
{\bf ct}(\bang_{s}A)={\bf ct}^{\prime}(\bang_{s}A)
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a family of sets of terms satisfying
(P1)-(P5)
, then

1.

{\bf ct}(A)
is a
\mathcal{P}
-candidate containing all stubborn terms of
{\bf P}_{A}
.

2.

Every
{\bf ct}(A)
is closed under substitution:
let
M\in{\bf ct}(A)
and let
S:\operatorname{Var}\to\operatorname{Term}

be a substitution such that
S(x)\in{\bf ct}(B)
for every
x:B\in{\bf ct}(A)
,
then
S(M)\in{\bf ct}(A)
.

Besides, for each
A
,
{\bf P}_{A}=\operatorname{Term}_{A}
.

Proof.

The proof of the first part is analogous to
[
Bie94
, Lemma 4, §5.1]
.
The proof of the second part is by induction on the length of
M
, let us consider some of the subexponential cases.

1.

Consider
\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}
. By the induction hypothesis, one has

S_{i}(M_{i})\in{\bf ct}(\bang_{s_{i}}A_{i})

for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\succeq s
and
S(M^{\prime})\in{\bf ct}(A)
.
So by
\mathcal{P}1
,
S_{i}(M_{i})\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s_{i}}A_{i}}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}

and
S(M)\in{\bf P}_{A}
.
If
\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}=S_{1}(M_{1}),\ldots,S_{n}(M_{n})
is stubborn,
then
\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}=S_{1}(M_{1}),\ldots,S_{n}(M_{n})\in{\bf ct}(\bang_{s}A)
by
\mathcal{P}3
.
Otherwise one has

\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}=S_{1}(M_{1}),\ldots,S_{n}(M_{n})\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}=M^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,M^{\prime}_{n}

By
(\mathcal{P}2)
, we have
M^{\prime}\in{\bf ct}(A)
. Also, we have
M^{\prime}_{1}\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\ldots,M^{\prime}_{n}\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s_{n}}A_{n}}

by
(P2)
. Thus
\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}=S_{1}(M_{1}),\ldots,S_{n}(M_{n})\in{\bf ct}(\bang_{s}A)
.
Therefore, we conclude that

S_{1};\ldots;S_{n};S(\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M})\in{\bf ct}(A).

where
S_{1};\ldots;S_{n};S
is the composite substitution.

2.

Consider
{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=M\>{\bf in}\>N
. By the induction hypothesis,

S_{1}(M)\in{\bf ct}(\bang_{s}A)
for
s\in C
and
S_{2}(N)\in{\bf ct}(B)
. Observe that
S_{2}
has the form of

S^{\prime}_{2}\cup\{x\mapsto P,y\mapsto Q\}
for some substitution
S^{\prime}_{2}
and for some
P,Q\in{\bf ct}(\bang_{s}A)
.

If
S_{1}(M)
is stubborn, then
{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y\>=S_{1}(M)\>{\bf in}\>S^{\prime}_{2}(N)\in{\bf ct}(B)
.
Otherwise,
S_{1}(M)\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{w}=\vec{Q}
.
By
(\mathcal{P}2)
,
\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{w}=\vec{Q}\in{\bf P}_{\bang_{s}A}
.
Therefore, we have

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{Q}\>{\bf in}\>S^{\prime}_{2}(N)[x:=\bang_{s}R\>{\bf with}\>\vec{w}=\vec{u},y:=\bang_{s}R\>{\bf with}\>\vec{w}=\vec{v}]\in{\bf P}_{B}

So by (P5),
{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y\>=S_{1}(M)\>{\bf in}\>S_{2}(N)\in{\bf P}_{B}
. By the definition,

{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y\>=S_{1}(M)\>{\bf in}\>S_{2}(N)\in{\bf ct}(B)
.
Thus
S_{1};S^{\prime}_{2}({\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=\>M\>{\bf in}\>N)\in{\bf ct}(B)
.

Now, clearly
{\bf P}_{A}\subseteq\operatorname{Term}_{A}
. For the converse inclusion, take any term
M

of type
A
, then
M\in{\bf ct}(A)
by the applying the identity substitution.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

Let
\operatorname{SN}_{A}
stand for the set of all strongly normalising terms of type
A
.
Now let
\mathcal{P}
be a family
\{\operatorname{SN}_{A}\>|\>A\in\operatorname{Type}\}
.
The rest is to show that
\mathcal{P}
satisfies the conditions (P1)-(P5).

Let us associate the reduction tree

{\bf rt}(M)=\{p\>|\>\text{$p$ is a reduction path starting at $M$}\}

with every term
M\in\operatorname{SN}_{A}
and every
such a tree satisfies König’s lemma (see
[
Hod93
, Exercise 5.6.5]
): every term has
a finite number of redexes and
\operatorname{length}(p)<\omega
for each
p\in{\bf rt}(M)
.
The proof is by induction on
d(M)
, the depth of
{\bf rt}(M)
.
So the rest is to check the conditions (P1)-(P5). We consider the most demonstrative cases with subexponentials.

(P3)

Let
s\in C
. Assume
M\in\operatorname{SN}_{\bang_{s}A}
,

N\in\operatorname{SN}_{B}
such that

{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=(\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M})\>{\bf in}\>N\in\operatorname{SN}_{B}

for
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}
.
We need
{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=M\>{\bf in}\>N\in\operatorname{SN}_{B}
. Take
{\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=M\>{\bf in}\>N\triangleleft_{\beta}P
.
If
P={\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=M_{1}\>{\bf in}\>N
, where
M_{1}
is simple and
M\triangleleft_{\beta}M_{1}
.
In this case, we apply the induction hypothesis for
d(M_{1})+d(N)
and conclude
P\in\operatorname{SN}_{\bang_{s}A}
.

If, otherwise,
P={\bf let}_{s}\>x,y=M\>{\bf in}\>N_{1}
, where
N_{1}
is simple and

N\triangleleft_{\beta}N_{1}
. Similarly, we apply the induction hypothesis for
d(M)+d(N_{1})
and conclude

P\in\operatorname{SN}_{\bang_{s}A}
.

(P4)

Let
M\in\operatorname{SN}_{A}
and let

M_{1}\in\operatorname{SN}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A},\ldots,M_{n}\in\operatorname{SN}_{\bang_{s_{n}}A}
.
Consider
\bang_{s}\>M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}N
for
s\preceq s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
.
Then
N
has the form of
\bang_{s}\>M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M^{\prime}}

for
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}M^{\prime}
,
M_{1}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}M^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}M^{\prime}_{n}
.
Then we have
\bang_{s}\>M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}\in\operatorname{SN}_{\bang_{s}A}
by induction on

d(M)+d(M_{1})+\ldots+d(M_{n})
.

(P5)

Let
s\in C
,
M\in\operatorname{SN}_{\bang_{s}A}
and
N\in\operatorname{SN}_{B}
such that

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M^{\prime}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]\in\operatorname{SN}_{B}

for
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M}
. We need
{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M\>{\bf in}\>N\in\operatorname{SN}_{B}
.
Consider
{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M\>{\bf in}\>N\triangleleft_{\beta}P
. As above, we have several alternatives:
Assume
P={\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M_{1}\>{\bf in}\>N
for
M\triangleleft_{\beta}M_{1}
,
then, as earlier, we apply the induction hypothesis on
d(M_{1})+d(N)
and conclude that
P\in\operatorname{SN}_{B}
.
The case
P={\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M\>{\bf in}\>N_{1}
for
N\triangleleft_{\beta}N_{1}
is considered similarly.
If
P={\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=(\bang_{s}M^{\prime}{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M})\>{\bf in}\>N
, then we are already done by assumption.

∎

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
M,N,P
be terms such that
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}N
and
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P
,
then there is a term
P^{\prime}
such that
N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P^{\prime}
and
P\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P^{\prime}
.
As a corollary, a normal form is unique up
\alpha
-equivalence.

Proof.

The proof strategy is similar to the strong normalisation proof.
Let
\operatorname{CR}_{A}
be the set of all confluent terms of type
A
and
let
\mathcal{P}
be
\{\operatorname{CR}_{A}\>|\>\text{$A$ is a type }\}
.
The goal is to show that
\mathcal{P}
satisfies (P1)-(P5) and then conclude
that every term is confluent by Lemma
2.10
. Let us consider one of the cases, in particular,
(P5)

for the polymodal. The rest of the clauses are proved similarly.

Let
s\in C
,
M\in\operatorname{CR}_{\bang_{s}A}
and
N\in\operatorname{CR}_{B}
such that
\operatorname{CR}_{B}
contains the following redex:

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M^{\prime}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]

for
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M^{\prime}}
. We need
{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M\>{\bf in}\>N\in\operatorname{CR}_{B}
.

There are the following subcases to show:

1.

Assume there are the following independent reductions:

{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M\>{\bf in}\>N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M_{1}\>{\bf in}\>N_{1},{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M_{2}\>{\bf in}\>N_{2}

for
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}M_{1},M_{2}
and
N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}N_{1},N_{2}
.
Both
M
and
N
are confluent by the assumption, so there are
M^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}

such that
M_{1},M_{2}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}M^{\prime}
and
N_{1},N_{2}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}N^{\prime}
.
Therefore,

{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M_{1}\>{\bf in}\>N_{1},{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M_{2}\>{\bf in}\>N_{2}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M^{\prime}\>{\bf in}\>N^{\prime}

2.

Assume there are the following top-level reduction:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M\>{\bf in}\>N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=(\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M_{1}})\>{\bf in}\>N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}&\\
&\>\>\>\>{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M_{1}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P_{1},&\\
&{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M\>{\bf in}\>N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=(\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M_{2}})\>{\bf in}\>N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}&\\
&\>\>\>\>{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M_{2}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P_{2}&\\
\end{array}

M
and
N
are already confluent, so there is a term
{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M^{\prime}\>{\bf in}\>N^{\prime}

such that:

{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=(\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M_{1}})\>{\bf in}\>N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M^{\prime}\>{\bf in}\>N^{\prime}

{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=(\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M_{2}})\>{\bf in}\>N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M^{\prime}\>{\bf in}\>N^{\prime}

M^{\prime}
, in turn, is of the form
\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{P}
.
Then we have the following reduction:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=(\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{P})\>{\bf in}\>N^{\prime}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}&\\
&\>\>\>\>{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{P}\>{\bf with}\>N^{\prime}[y:=\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]&\end{array}

By assumption, the following terms are confluent:

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M_{1}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}],

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M_{2}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]

and we have the following reductions:

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M_{1}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]\trianglelefteq P_{1}

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M_{2}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]\trianglelefteq P_{2}

and we also have

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M_{1}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]\trianglelefteq{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{P}\>{\bf with}\>N^{\prime}[y:=\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M_{2}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]\trianglelefteq{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{P}\>{\bf with}\>N^{\prime}[y:=\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]

Therefore, we have
P^{\prime}
such that
P_{1}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P^{\prime}
and

\penalty\ {\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{P}\>{\bf with}\>N^{\prime}[y:=\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}P\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P^{\prime}.

(1)

Further, we have that

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M_{2}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M_{2}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P^{\prime},

so we have
P^{\prime}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P^{\prime\prime}
and
P_{2}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P^{\prime\prime}
.

3.

Assume we have the case when the first reduction is top-level, whereas the other one is independent (the symmetric case is considered similarly):

\begin{array}[]{lll}&{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M\>{\bf in}\>N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=(\bang_{s}M_{1}{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M_{1}})\>{\bf in}\>N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}&\\
&\>\>\>\>{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M_{1}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P_{1},&\\
&{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M\>{\bf in}\>N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M_{2}\>{\bf in}\>N_{2}=P_{2}&\end{array}

for
M\trianglelefteq_{\beta}M_{2}
and
N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}N_{2}
.
By the condition, both
M
and
N
are confluent, so there is a term

{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M^{\prime}\>{\bf in}\>N^{\prime}

such that there are the following reductions:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=(\bang_{s}M_{1}{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M_{1}})\>{\bf in}\>N\trianglelefteq_{\beta}{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M^{\prime}\>{\bf in}\>N^{\prime}&\\
&{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M_{2}\>{\bf in}\>N_{2}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=M^{\prime}\>{\bf in}\>N^{\prime}&\end{array}

Further,
M^{\prime}
has the form of
\bang_{s}M^{\prime\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M^{\prime\prime}}
, so there is the following reduction:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&{\bf let}_{s}\>y,z=(\bang_{s}M^{\prime\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{M^{\prime\prime}})\>{\bf in}\>N^{\prime}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}&\\
&\>\>\>\>{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M^{\prime\prime}}\>{\bf in}\>N^{\prime}[y:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]&\end{array}

By the assumption, the term

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M_{1}}\>{\bf in}\>N[y:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M_{1}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]

is confluent. So there is a term
P_{3}
such that
P_{1}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P_{3}

and

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M^{\prime\prime}}\>{\bf in}\>N^{\prime}[y:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P_{3}.

The following term

{\bf let}_{s}\>\vec{u},\vec{v}=\vec{M^{\prime\prime}}\>{\bf in}\>N_{2}[y:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{u},z:=\bang_{s}M^{\prime}\>{\bf with}\>\vec{x}=\vec{v}]

is confluent, so there is a term
P_{4}
such that
P_{2}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P_{4}
and
P_{3}\trianglelefteq_{\beta}P_{4}
.

∎

3
Category-theoretic Preliminaries

In this section, we construct the categorical denotational semantics for the type theory we introduced in the previous section.
We introduce Cocteau categories and assemblages to have an adequate semantic interpretation of
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{3}

and
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
respectively.
We assume that the reader is familiar with the underlying notions of category theory such as (1-)categories, functors, natural
transformations and adjunctions.

In further sections, we will obtain some results about categories of generally large categories and such objects
cannot be defined in the standard set-theoretic foundations of mathematics such as ZFC or NBG, so we accept the foundations based
on Grothendieck universes as they are described in
[
Lur09
, §1.2.15]
or
[
Shu08
, §8]
.

3.1
2-categories

We refer the reader to
[
JY21
, Chapter 2]
,
[
Lac09
]
and
[
Bor94a
, Chapter 7]

for a more thorough and systematic introduction.

Definition 3.1
.

A 2-category
\mathcal{C}
consists of the following data:

•

The class of objects (also called
0-cells
)
\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
,

•

For each pair of 0-cells
A,B\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
there is a class of morphisms (also called
1-cells
)
\mathcal{C}(A,B)

such that for each
A,B,C\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})

there is a composition map
g\circ f\in\mathcal{C}(A,C)
for
f\in\mathcal{C}(A,B)
and
g\in\mathcal{C}(B,C)
and
there is the identity map
1_{A}\in\mathcal{C}(A,A)
. The composition operation and identity morphisms satisfy the standard axioms.

•

For each 0-cells
A,B\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
and for each 1-cells
f,g\in{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
, the class
\mathcal{C}(f,g)
of

2-cells
between
f
and
g
is defined. The notation
\alpha:f\Rightarrow g
stands for
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}(f,g)
. 2-cells
are visualised as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

•

Let
\alpha:f\Rightarrow g
and
\beta:g\Rightarrow h
for
f,g\in\mathcal{C}(A,B)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

then there is the

vertical composite
of
\alpha
and
\beta
, which is a 2-cell
\beta\alpha
represented diagrammatically
as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\alpha}

•

Let
\alpha_{1}:f_{1}\Rightarrow f_{2}
and
\alpha_{2}:g_{1}\Rightarrow g_{2}

for
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(A,B)
and
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(B,C)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}

then
there is the
horizontal composite
of
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
, a 2-cell denoted as

\alpha_{2}\circ\alpha_{1}:g_{1}\circ f_{1}\Rightarrow g_{2}\circ f_{2}
represented diagramatically as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}\circ f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}\circ f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}\circ\alpha_{1}}

•

The vertical and horizontal composites should satisfy the interchange laws that one can find in the aforementioned
references and there are two identity two cells: the vertical 2-cell
1_{f}:f\Rightarrow f
for any
f

and the horizontal 2-cell
id_{A}:1_{A}\Rightarrow 1_{A}
for any 0-cell
A
.

In a 2-category
\mathcal{C}
, every
\mathcal{C}(A,B)
is a category itself for any 0-cells
A,B
.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be 2-categories, a
strict 2-functor

\mathsf{F}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
consists of the following data:

•

A class function
\mathsf{F}:\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{D})
,

•

Given
A,B\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
, there is a functor

\mathsf{F}_{A,B}:\mathcal{C}(A,B)\to\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{F}A,\mathsf{F}B)
.

•

There are identities for each
A,B,C\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
:

–

1_{\mathsf{F}A}=\mathsf{F}_{A,A}({\bf id_{A}})
,

–

\mathsf{F}_{A,C}(g\circ f)=\mathsf{F}_{B,C}(g)\circ\mathsf{F}_{A,B}(f)
for each 1-cells
f:A\to B
and
g:B\to C
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 3.3
.

{\bf Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is a 2-category consisting of the following data:

•

0-cells are strict 2-functors,

•

\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be 2-functors,
then 1-cells are
strict natural transformations

\eta:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}
,

•

Let
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}
be 1-cells, then the class of 2-cells
between
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are
modifications

\eta_{1}\Rightarrow\eta_{2}
.

Both strict natural transformations and their modifications are given accurately in, for example,
[
JY21
, §4.4]
.

Let us recall how one treats adjunctions in a 2-categorical setting, the reader can find more details in
[
KS06
, §2]

and also
[
Kal20
, Section 3]
and
[
RV16
]
for more advanced aspects of 2-categorical
adjunctions.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 2-category and let
A,B\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
be 0-cells, an
adjuction
in
\mathcal{C}
, denoted as
\eta,\varepsilon:f_{l}\dashv f_{r}:A\to B

(or just
f_{l}\dashv f_{r}
, when it is clear from the context what other components of an adjunction are),
consists of a pair of 1-cells
f_{r}:A\to B
and
f_{l}:B\to A
and a pair of 2-cells
\eta:1_{B}\Rightarrow f_{r}\circ f_{l}

and
\varepsilon:f_{l}\circ f_{r}\Rightarrow 1_{A}
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{f_{r}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{f_{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

We say that
f_{l}
is
left adjoint
to
f_{r}
and
f_{r}
is
right adjoint
to
f_{l}
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 2-category, let
A,B
be 0-cells and let
\eta,\varepsilon:f_{l}\dashv f_{r}:A\to B
and

\eta^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}:f^{\prime}_{l}\dashv f^{\prime}_{r}:A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
be adjunctions in
\mathcal{C}
, then a
left morphism of adjunctions
consists
a pair of 1-cells
g_{1}:A\to A^{\prime}
and
g_{2}:B\to B^{\prime}
such that
g_{1}\circ f_{l}=f^{\prime}_{l}\circ g_{2}
.

Note that there are different non-equivalent definitions of an adjunction morphism. For example,
the definition of an adjunction morphism given in
[
Rie17
, Exercise 4.2.v]
extends our definition of left
adjunction morphism with a similar extra equality of composites with right adjoints. Whereas,
the definition of an adjunction morphism from
[
Zag17
, Definition 2.2.2]
contains only the equality
with right adjoints. So we intended to coin the term “left adjunction morphism” to avoid further termilogical
confusion.

Definition 3.6
.

Given a 2-category
\mathcal{C}
, a 2-category
{\bf Adj}^{l}(\mathcal{C})

of adjunctions in
\mathcal{C}
and their left morphisms is defined as
the full 1,2-category of
{\bf Fun}([1],\mathcal{C})
spanned by 1-cells in
\mathcal{C}
having a right adjoint.

[1]
denotes a two-element category having two objects
0
and
1
and a single non-identity morphism

0\to 1
.

Comonads in 2-categories are defined by the dualisation of the corresponding definition of a monad in 2-categories from
[
Str72
]
.
The formal theory of comonads can be derived by dualising the formal theory of monads. The
reader can find the explicit presentation in
[
Zwa24
, §2.3]
.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 2-category, a
comonad
in
\mathcal{C}
consists of a 0-cell
A\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
along with
a 1-cell
k:A\to A
and 2-cells
\delta:k\Rightarrow k\circ k
(
comultiplication
) and
\varepsilon:k\Rightarrow 1_{A}

(
counit
) such that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces kk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces kk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces kk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces kkk}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta k}

The dual notion is the notion of a monad, a triple
(j,\eta,\mu)
, where
\eta:1\Rightarrow j
and
\mu:jj\Rightarrow j
are
2-cells called
unit
and
multiplication
respectively satisfying the diagrams dual to the above ones.

Most of the time, we will identify a comonad
(k,\delta,\varepsilon)
over
A\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})

with the carrier arrow
k
. A usual (co)monad over a category
\mathcal{C}
is a (co)monad in the 2-category
{\bf Cat}

of categories, functors and natural transformations. It is well known
that every adjunction in a 2-category gives a comonad. Indeed, let

\eta,\varepsilon:f_{l}\dashv f_{r}:A\to B
be an adjunction in
\mathcal{C}
,
then the composite
f_{l}\circ f_{r}:A\to A
forms a comonad on
A

with the counit 2-cell
\varepsilon:f_{l}\circ f_{r}\Rightarrow 1_{A}

and the comultiplication 2-cell
f_{l}\eta f_{r}:f_{l}\circ f_{r}\Rightarrow f_{l}\circ f_{r}\circ f_{l}\circ f_{r}
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
(A,k)
and
(B,l)
be comonads in a 2-category
\mathcal{C}
, a
comonad morphism

(f,\mu):(A,k)\to(B,l)
consists of a 1-cell
f:A\to B
and a 2-cell
\mu:fk\Rightarrow lf

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces lf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces lf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fkk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces lfk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces llf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\mu}

The next is to discuss how left adjunction morphisms induce comonad morphisms. For that, let us recall the notion of a mate.
Let
\eta,\varepsilon:f_{l}\dashv f_{r}:A\to B
and
\eta^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}:f^{\prime}_{l}\dashv f^{\prime}_{r}:A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
be adjunctions in
\mathcal{C}
.
Let
g_{1}:A\to A^{\prime}
and
g_{2}:B\to B^{\prime}
, then there is a bijection between 2-cells

f_{l}^{\prime}\circ g_{1}\Rightarrow g_{2}\circ f_{l}
and
g_{1}\circ f_{r}\Rightarrow f^{\prime}_{r}\circ g_{2}
, so squares of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}}

1-to-1 correspond to squares of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{r}}

So those pairs of 2-cells are called
mates
, see
[
Lac09
, §2]
and
[
KS06
, §2]
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 2-category, and let
\eta,\varepsilon:f_{l}\dashv f_{r}:A\to B
and

\eta^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}:f^{\prime}_{l}\dashv f^{\prime}_{r}:A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
be adjunctions in
\mathcal{C}

and let
g_{1}:A\to A^{\prime}
and
g_{2}:B\to B^{\prime}
be 1-cells such that a pair
(g_{1},g_{2})
is a left morphism
of the corresponding adjunctions. Then
(g_{1},g_{2})
induces a morphism of comonads
f_{l}\circ f_{r}\Rightarrow f^{\prime}_{l}\circ f^{\prime}_{r}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to
[
Zag17
, Proposition 2.2.4]
,
but let us just demonstrate how exactly a comonad morphism is given.
Let
\beta:g_{2}f_{r}\Rightarrow f^{\prime}_{r}g_{1}
be the mate of the identity
g_{1}f_{l}=f_{l}^{\prime}g_{2}
, that is,
the following composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

Then a pair
(g_{1},f^{\prime}_{l}\beta)
is a comonad morphism
f_{l}f_{r}\Rightarrow f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}
.
∎

Definition 3.10
.

Let
(A,k_{1})
and
(B,k_{2})
be comonads in a 2-category
\mathcal{C}

and let
(f_{1},\mu_{1}),(f_{2},\mu_{2}):k_{1}\Rightarrow k_{2}
be comonad morphisms,
a
comonad morphism transformation
is a 2-cell
\varphi:f_{1}\Rightarrow f_{2}
such that the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}k_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}k_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{2}f_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{2}f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi k_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{2}\varphi}

Taking Definition
3.8
and Definition
3.10
together, we define
the 2-category
{\bf coMnd}(\mathcal{C})
consisting of all comonads in a 2-category
\mathcal{C}
,
their morphisms and morphism transformations.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\eta,\varepsilon:f_{l}\dashv f_{r}
and
\eta^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}:f^{\prime}_{l}\dashv f^{\prime}_{r}

be adjunctions between
A
and
B
in a 2-category
\mathcal{C}
. Let
(g_{1},g_{2})
and
(g^{\prime}_{1},g^{\prime}_{2})

be left adjunction morphisms from
f_{l}\dashv f_{r}
and
f^{\prime}_{l}\dashv f^{\prime}_{g}

inducing comonad morphisms
(g_{1},\mu_{1}):f_{l}f_{r}\Rightarrow f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}
and
(g^{\prime}_{1},\mu_{2}):f_{l}f_{r}\Rightarrow f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}
respectively and
let
\alpha_{1}:g_{1}\Rightarrow g^{\prime}_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}:g_{2}\Rightarrow g^{\prime}_{2}
be 2-cells in
\mathcal{C}
such that

\alpha_{1}f_{l}=f^{\prime}_{l}\alpha_{2}
. Then
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
induce a comonad morphism transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}f_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{1},\mu_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g^{\prime}_{1},\mu_{2})}

Proof.

Let us show that the following square of 2-cells commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}f_{l}f_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}_{1}f_{l}f_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}g_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}g^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}f_{l}f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}\alpha_{1}}

(2)

By Proposition
3.9
, we can unveil
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
as

f^{\prime}_{l}\beta:f^{\prime}_{l}g_{2}f_{r}\Rightarrow f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}g_{1}
and

f^{\prime}_{l}\beta^{\prime}:f^{\prime}_{l}g^{\prime}_{2}f_{r}\Rightarrow f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}g^{\prime}_{1}
. So let us rewrite (
2
)
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}f_{l}f_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}_{1}f_{l}f_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}g_{2}f_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}g^{\prime}_{2}f_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}g_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}g^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}f_{l}f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}\alpha_{2}f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}f^{\prime}_{r}\alpha_{1}}

(3)

Observe that the top square commutes by
\alpha_{1}f_{l}=f^{\prime}_{l}\alpha_{2}
. Whereas the bottom one is unveiled as follows and it holds
by the assumption as well:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}

∎

So we have a 2-functor from
{\bf Adj}^{l}(\mathcal{C})
to
{\bf coMnd}(\mathcal{C})
by
Proposition
3.9
and Proposition
3.11

But there is the other way round 2-functor as well, but for 2-categories admitting the construction of coalgebras,
so we need a couple of more definitions.

We assume that the classical notion of a coalgebra over a comonad is already known to the reader,
but we refer to
[
BW00
, Chapter 3]
to have introduction to algebras over a monad, which is applicable
to comonads and coalgebras by duality.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 2-category and let
(A,k)
be a comonad in
\mathcal{C}
. An
object of coalgebras
(also
called a
co-Eilenberg-Moore object
) is a 0-cell
A^{k}\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
(if it exists) such that for each
0-cell
B
:

\penalty\ \mathcal{C}(B,A^{k})\cong{\bf coMnd}(\mathcal{C})(1_{B},k).

(4)

\mathcal{C}

admits the construction of coalgebras
if every comonad has an object of coalgebras.

Fix a comonad
(A,k)
in a 2-category
\mathcal{C}
with an object of coalgebras
A^{k}
. We have a comonad morphism

(k,\delta):(A,1_{A})\to(A,k)
, so we obtain
the cofree morphism
in
v:A\to A^{k}
, a 1-cell in
\mathcal{C}
,
by transposing
(k,\delta)

through (
4
). Whereas
the coforgetful morphism

u:A^{k}\to A
is obtain by transposing the identity
morphism
1_{A^{k}}:A^{k}\to A^{k}
. Thus we have an adjunction
u\dashv v:A\to A^{k}
in
\mathcal{C}
which is called
the
cofree-coforgetful decomposition
.

Let
f_{l}\dashv f_{r}:C\to D
be an adjunction in
\mathcal{C}
decomposing a comonad
(A,k)

as the composite
f_{l}f_{r}
. The
comparison morphism

g:B\to A^{k}
is obtained by transposing
the comonad morphism
(f_{l},f_{l}\eta):(C,1_{C})\to(C,k)
. An adjunction is
comonadic
is the comparison map is
isomorphism.

Therefore, we have (the complete proof can be adapted from
[
Str72
, Theorem 2]
):

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 2-category that admits the construction of coalgebras, then

{\bf coMnd}(\mathcal{C})
fully faithfully embeds to
{\bf Adj}^{l}(\mathcal{C})
.

3.2
Symmetric Monoidal Categories, Cosemigroups and Comonoids

We also recall underlying definitions and facts related to symmetric monoidal categories.
We refer to
[
EGNO15
, Chapter 2]
for a more systematic introduction to the topic.

Definition 3.14
.

A
symmetric monoidal category
(SMC) is a category
\mathcal{C}
equipped
with a bifunctor
\underline{\>\>}\otimes\underline{\>\>}:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

called
tensor product
, the unit object
\mathds{1}
along with the following natural isomorphisms
for each
A,B,C\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
:

•

\alpha_{A,B,C}:(A\otimes B)\otimes C\cong A\otimes(B\otimes C)
,

•

\rho_{A}:\mathds{1}\otimes A\cong A\cong A\otimes\mathds{1}:\lambda_{A}
,

•

\sigma_{A,B}:A\otimes B\cong B\otimes A
.

Those isomorphisms are connected with each other by the well-known axioms
that one can find in
[
ML13
, §VII.1, §XI.1]
.

Definition 3.15
.

A symmetric monoidal category is
closed

2
2

2

The abbrevation SMCC stands for a “symmetric closed monoidal category”.
if for each
A\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})

the functor
B\mapsto A\otimes B
has a right adjoint
B\mapsto[A,B]
.
In other words, for each
A,B\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
there is
[A,B]\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
(the internal Hom of
A
and
B
)
along with an arrow
{\bf ev}_{A,B}:[A,B]\otimes A\to B
such that
for each
f:C\otimes A\to B
there exists a unique
\hat{f}:C\to[A,B]
such that
the following triangle commutes:

\textstyle{[A,B]\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\bf ev}_{A,B}}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{f}\otimes 1_{A}}
\scriptstyle{f}

Comonoid objects are rather common in category theory, see
[
AM10
, §1.2]
.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1})
be a monoidal category, then

•

A
cosemigroup object
in
\mathcal{C}
is an object

A\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
with a choice of a morphism
\gamma_{A}:A\to A\otimes A
called
comultiplication
such that the coassociativity axiom is satisfied:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes(A\otimes A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}\otimes\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A,A,A}\circ(\gamma_{A}\otimes 1_{A})}

•

A
morphism of cosemigroups

f:(A,\gamma_{A})\to(B,\gamma_{B})
is an arrow
f:A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
making the below square commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}

The coassociativity axiom means that the comultiplication must respect the associator canonically.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1})
be an SMC, then a cosemigroup
(A,\gamma_{A})
is
cocommutative
if
the extra axiom is satisfied:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{A,A}}

{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})
is the category of cocommutative cosemigroups in
\mathcal{C}
and their morphisms.

Similarly, the cocommutativity axiom is a requirement making the cosemigroup comultiplication commute with the structure
of a symmetric monoidal category.

Definition 3.18
.

A
comonoid
is a cosemigroup object
(A,\gamma)
along with a choice of a morphism
\iota_{A}:A\to\mathds{1}
(
counit
)
commuting with the comulplication operation the following way:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{A}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{A}\otimes 1_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}\otimes\iota_{A}}

A
comonoid morphism
is a cosemigroup morphism
f:(A,\gamma_{A})\to(B,\gamma_{B})
preserving the counit:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{B}}

A comonoid is
cocommutative
if its comultiplication is commutative.

{\bf coMon}(\mathcal{C})
denotes the category of cocommutative comultiplication comonoids and their morphisms.

The following statement is rather well-known:

Theorem 3.19
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1})
be a symmetric monoidal category, then
the categories of cocommutative cosemigroups and comonoids
{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})
and
{\bf coMon}(\mathcal{C})

are symmetric monoidal with the following structure:

•

The tensor product of cosemigroups
(A,\gamma_{A})
and
(B,\gamma_{B})
is the cosemigroup
(A\otimes B,\gamma_{A\otimes B})

where
\gamma_{A\otimes B}:A\otimes B\to(A\otimes B)\otimes(A\otimes B)
is given by the composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes B)\otimes(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes A)\otimes(B\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes(B\otimes(A\otimes B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes(A\otimes(B\otimes B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes((B\otimes A)\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes((A\otimes B)\otimes B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A\otimes B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}\otimes\gamma_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A,A,B\otimes B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}_{A,B,A\otimes B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}\otimes\alpha^{-1}_{A,B,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}\otimes\alpha_{B,A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}\otimes(\sigma_{A,B}\otimes 1_{B})}

In
{\bf coMon}(\mathcal{C})
, the tensor product of comonoids
(A,\gamma_{A},\iota_{A})
and
(B,\gamma_{B},\iota_{B})
is
the comonoid
(A\otimes B,\gamma_{A\otimes B},\iota_{A\otimes B})
where the counit
\iota_{A\otimes B}

is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{A}\otimes\iota_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda=\rho}

•

The monoidal unit in
{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})
is the cosemigroup

(\mathds{1},\gamma_{\mathds{1}})
where
\gamma_{\mathds{1}}
is just
\rho^{-1}
. In the category of comonoids,
the cosemigroup
(\mathds{1},\gamma_{\mathds{1}})
is equipped with the identity map

1_{\mathds{1}}:\mathds{1}\to\mathds{1}
as the counit morphism.

Moreover, the forgetful functors
{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}

and
{\bf coMon}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
are strict monoidal and symmetric.

The notions of a monoid and a semigroup are obtained by the dualisation of the above definitions,
see
[
Por08
]
to have a more thorough introduction to categorical (co)monoids.

Definition 3.20
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be symmetric monoidal categories, then
a
symmetric lax monoidal functor
is a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
equipped with

•

A morphism
\mathsf{m}_{\mathds{1}}:\mathds{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{F}\mathds{1}
,

•

A natural transformation
\mathsf{m}
with the components
\mathsf{m}_{A,B}:\mathsf{F}A\otimes\mathsf{F}B\rightarrow\mathsf{F}(A\otimes B)

for
