A,B\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
.

All those natural transformations should satisfy the commutative diagrams from, e.g.,
[
AM10
, Chapter 3]
.
A tuple
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{m}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
stands for a
\mathsf{F}
is a symmetric lax monoidal functor from
\mathcal{C}

to
\mathcal{D}
with the natural transformation
\mathsf{m}
.
\mathsf{F}
is strong if its components are natural
isomorphisms and is strict if the components are identity morphisms.

\mathsf{F}
is
strong
if the components
\mathsf{m}_{A,B}
and
\mathsf{m}_{\mathds{1}}

are isomorphisms for each
A,B\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
.
\mathsf{F}
is
strict
if all those components are identities.

Dually, a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is
symmetric oplax monoidal
if it is equipped with

•

A morphism
\mathsf{n}_{\mathds{1}}:\mathsf{F}\mathds{1}\to\mathds{1}
,

•

A natural transformation
\mathsf{m}
with the components
\mathsf{n}_{A,B}:\mathsf{F}(A\otimes B)\rightarrow\mathsf{F}A\otimes\mathsf{F}B
.
for
A,B\in\operatorname{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
.

such that they satisfy the conditions dual to Definition
3.20
.

Definition 3.21
.

Let
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{m}),(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{n}):\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
be symmetric lax monoidal functors,
then a natural transformation
\eta:(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{m})\Rightarrow(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{n})
is monoidal if the following diagrams commute
for each
A,B\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}A\otimes\mathsf{F}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}A\otimes\mathsf{G}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}\otimes\eta_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A\otimes B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{\mathds{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{\mathds{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathds{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{n}_{A,B}}

{\bf SymmMonCat}
is a 2-category of symmetric monoidal categories, symmetric lax monoidal natural transformations
and symmetric lax monoidal natural transformations.

{\bf SymmMonCat}_{\operatorname{strict}}
is a 2-subcategory
{\bf SymmMonCat}
where 1-cells are strict symmetric monoidal functors.

A
symmetric monoidal adjunction
can be defined as an adjunction in the 2-category
{\bf SymmMonCat}
(see the definition of
{\bf SymmMonCat}
),
but there is an explicit characterisation of symmetric monoidal adjunctions, see
[
Kel06
]
:

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1})
and

(\mathcal{D},\otimes,\mathds{1})
be SMCs and let

(\mathsf{F}_{l},\mathsf{n}):(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1})\to(\mathcal{D},\otimes,\mathds{1})

be a symmetric lax monoidal functor.
Assume that
\mathsf{F}_{l}
is a left adjoint to
\mathsf{F}_{r}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
. Then the
adjunction
\mathsf{F}_{l}\dashv\mathsf{F}_{r}
lifts to a monoidal adjunction
(\mathsf{F}_{l},\mathsf{n})\dashv(\mathsf{F}_{r},\mathsf{o})

iff
\mathsf{F}_{l}
is strong. In this case, the components
\mathsf{o}_{A,B}:\mathsf{F}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{F}_{r}B\to\mathsf{F}_{r}(A\otimes B)

and
\mathsf{o}_{\mathds{1}}:\mathds{1}\to\mathsf{F}_{r}\mathds{1}
are given by:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{F}_{r}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{r}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{r}\mathsf{F}_{l}(\mathsf{F}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{F}_{r}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{r}(\mathsf{F}_{l}\mathsf{F}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{F}_{l}\mathsf{F}_{r}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{r}\mathsf{F}_{l}\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{r}\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{o}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathsf{F}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{F}_{r}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{r}(\mathsf{n}^{-1}_{\mathsf{F}_{r}A,\mathsf{F}_{r}B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{r}(\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\varepsilon_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathds{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{n}^{-1}_{\mathds{1}}}

In particular, in any symmetric monoidal adjunction
(\mathsf{F}_{l},\mathsf{m})\dashv(\mathsf{F}_{r},\mathsf{n})
,
a left adjoint
\mathsf{F}_{l}
is strong.

Definition 3.23
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1})
be a symmetric monoidal category and let
(\mathsf{K},\varepsilon,\delta)

be a comonad on
\mathcal{C}
, then the structure of a
symmetric lax monoidal comonad
on
\mathsf{K}
is given by the
structure of a symmetric lax monoidal functor
(\mathsf{K},\mathsf{m})
such that the following diagrams commute for each
A,B\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{2}\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{2}A\otimes\mathsf{K}^{2}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{2}(A\otimes B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{\mathds{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{\mathds{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathds{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{A}\otimes\delta_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{A\otimes B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{m}_{\mathds{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{K}A,\mathsf{K}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{m}_{A,B})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\varepsilon_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A\otimes B}}

A
symmetric lax monoidal comonad morphism
of symmetric lax monoidal comonads is a comonad morphism in
{\bf Cmd}({\bf SymmMonCat})
. Explicitly,
the structure of a symmetric lax monoidal comonad morphism given by a comonad morphism with the structure of a symmetric
lax monoidal natural transformation.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal category and let
(\mathsf{K},\mathsf{m}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

be a lax symmetric monoidal functor with the structure of a symmetric lax monoidal comonad. There is a
co-free co-forgetful adjunction between the underlying category
\mathcal{C}
and the Eilenberg-Moore category

\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{K}}
, which lifts to a symmetric monoidal adjunction if we equip the Eilenberg-Moore category
with a symmetric monoidal structure. Let
(A,h_{A})
and
(B,h_{B})
be
\mathsf{K}
-coalgebras from
\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{K}}
.
The tensor product of
(A,h_{A})
and
(B,h_{B})
is defined as a coalgebra
(A\otimes B,h_{A\otimes B})
where
the coalgebra action
h_{A\otimes B}

is given by the composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(A\otimes B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}\otimes h_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{A,B}}

And the unit coalgebra is defined as
(\mathds{1},\mathsf{m}_{\mathds{1}})
. Therefore, we have the following
(see
[
Lac05
]
for more details and further generalisations):

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal category and let
\mathsf{K}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

be a lax symmetric monoidal comonad, then the Eilenberg-Moore category is symmetric monoidal.

4
Assemblages and Semantic Interpretation of
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}

In categorical semantics of linear logic as it is studied in, for example,
[
AT11
]
and
[
Mel09
]
,
one can consider so-called
linear categories
, symmetric monoidal categories equipped with a symmetric lax
monoidal comonad that, informally speaking, assign a cocommutative comonoid with each object of an underlying category.
The heuristics is that we can associate the data type
\bang A
with each
A
such that elements of
\bang A
can be
both copied and destroyed. Such comonads allows interpreting the exponential modality semantically.
We below suggest the notion of a Cocteau category by expanding linear categories with two extra comonads
for modelling those data types that can be destroyed and those that can be copied.

4.1
Cocteau Categories and Assemblages

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal category, then:

•

An
exponential
comonad is a symmetric monoidal comonad
(\mathsf{K},\varepsilon,\delta)
on
\mathcal{C}
with a pair
of symmetric monoidal natural transformations

\mathsf{c}:\mathsf{K}\Rightarrow\mathsf{K}(.)\otimes\mathsf{K}(.)
and
\mathsf{d}:\mathsf{K}\Rightarrow(\underline{\>\>\>}\mapsto\mathds{1})
such that
the following is satisfied:

–

(\mathsf{K}A,\mathsf{c}_{A},\mathsf{d}_{A})
is a cocommutative comonoid for each
A\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
,

–

\delta_{A}
is a comonoid morphism, i.e., the following axioms are satisfied:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{2}A\otimes\mathsf{K}^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{K}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{d}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{d}_{\mathsf{K}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{A}\otimes\delta_{A}}

–

\mathsf{c}_{A}
and
\mathsf{d}_{A}
are coalgebra morphisms, i.e., the following axioms are satisfied:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{2}A\otimes\mathsf{K}^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}\mathsf{c}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{d}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{d}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{A}\otimes\delta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{K}A,\mathsf{K}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{\mathds{1}}}

•

A
relevant
comonad is a symmetric monoidal comonad
\mathsf{K}
with a
symmetric monoidal natural transformation
\mathsf{c}:\mathsf{K}\Rightarrow\mathsf{K}(.)\otimes\mathsf{K}(.)

such that a pair
(\mathsf{K}A,\mathsf{c}_{A})
is a cocommutative cosemigroup,

\delta_{A}
is a cosemigroup morphism and
\mathsf{c}_{A}
is a coalgebra morphism.

•

((\mathsf{K},\mathsf{m}),\varepsilon,\delta,\mathsf{d})
is a
affine
comonad if it has a natural transformation
with the components
\mathsf{d}_{A}:\mathsf{K}A\to\mathds{1}
such that the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{d}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{d}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{\mathds{1}}}

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a SMCC category and let
(\bang_{\bf i},\mathsf{m}^{\bf i}),(\bang_{\bf a},\mathsf{m}^{\bf a}),(\bang_{\bf r},\mathsf{m}^{\bf r})
be lax symmetric monoidal endofunctors,
then a
Cocteau category
3
3

3

Cocteau categories are named after a French poet and director Jean Cocteau and his Orphic trilogy consisting of the
films
The Blood of a Poet
,
Orpheus
and
Testament of Orpheus
.
is given by the following data:

•

((\bang_{\bf i},\mathsf{m}^{\bf i}),\mathsf{c}^{\bf i},\mathsf{d}^{\bf i})
is an exponential comonad,

•

((\bang_{\bf a},\mathsf{m}^{\bf a}),\mathsf{d}^{\bf a})
is an affine comonad,

•

((\bang_{\bf r},\mathsf{m}^{\bf r}),\mathsf{c}^{\bf r})
is a relevant comonad,

•

\mu_{\bf ir}:\bang_{\bf i}\Rightarrow\bang_{\bf r}
and
\mu_{\bf ia}:\bang_{\bf i}\Rightarrow\bang_{\bf a}

are symmetric lax monoidal comonad morphisms such that the following diagrams commute for each
A\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf i}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf i}A\otimes\bang_{\bf i}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf i}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf a}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}A\otimes\bang_{\bf r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf i}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\bf ir}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\bf ir}\otimes\mu_{\bf ir}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\bf ia}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{d}^{\bf i}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{d}^{\bf a}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}_{A}}

Convention 4.3
.

In order to distinguish similar components of different comonads in a Cocteau category, we will sometimes label
them with an upper index as follows: for example,
\varepsilon^{\bf a}
will stand for
comultiplication in an affine comonad, whereas
\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}
will mean the copying
operation in a relevant comonad.

Example 4.4
.

One can extract a rather natural example of a Cocteau category from quantales,
see
[
EGHK18
]
for a more general context.

A
quantale
is a structure
\mathcal{Q}=(Q,\cdot,1,\bigvee)
where
(Q,\bigvee)
is a complete sup-semilattice
and
(Q,\cdot,1)
is a monoid such that the multiplication operation preserves suprema in each coordinate.
Any commutative quantale
\mathcal{Q}
is an SMCC with the internal
\operatorname{Hom}
given by

[a,b]=\bigvee\{c\in\mathcal{Q}\>|\>c\cdot a\leq b\}.

A
quantic conucleus
on a quantale
\mathcal{Q}
is a coclosure operator
g:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}

such that
g(a)\cdot g(b)\leq g(a\cdot b)
and
g(1)=1
for each
a,b\in\mathcal{Q}
.
This is a known fact that there is a bijection between subquantales of a quantale
and quantic conuclei: if
g:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}
is a quantic conucleus, then the set

\mathcal{Q}_{g}=\{a\in\mathcal{Q}\>|\>g(a)=a\}
forms a subquantale of
\mathcal{Q}
, see, for example,
[
Ros90
, Theorem 3.1.3]
. Moreover,
if
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\trianglelefteq\mathcal{Q}
is a subquantale, then there exists a quantic conucleus
g
such that
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.
Further, one can observe the subsets
\mathcal{Q}_{\bf i}=\{a\in\mathcal{Q}\>|\>a\leq 1\>\&\>a\leq a\cdot a\}
,

\mathcal{Q}_{\bf a}=\{a\in\mathcal{Q}\>|\>a\leq 1\}

and
\mathcal{Q}_{\bf r}=\{a\in\mathcal{Q}\>|\>a\leq a\cdot a\}
are subquantales of
\mathcal{Q}
.
so there are quantic conuclei
g_{\bf i}
,
g_{\bf a}
and
g_{\bf r}
such that
\mathcal{Q}_{\bf i}=\mathcal{Q}_{g_{\bf i}}
,

\mathcal{Q}_{\bf a}=\mathcal{Q}_{g_{\bf a}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\bf r}=\mathcal{Q}_{g_{\bf r}}
. For each
a\in\mathcal{Q}
one also has

g_{\bf i}(a)\leq 1
and
g_{\bf i}(a)\leq g_{\bf i}(a)\cdot g_{\bf i}(a)
,

g_{\bf r}(a)\leq g_{\bf r}(a)\cdot g_{\bf r}(a)
,
g_{\bf a}(a)\leq 1
.
Besides,
g_{\bf i}(a)\leq g_{\bf r}(a)
and
g_{\bf i}(a)\leq g_{\bf a}(a)
for each
a\in\mathcal{Q}
.
Thus, a commutative quantale
\mathcal{Q}
along with quantic conuclei
g_{\bf i}
,
g_{\bf a}
and
g_{\bf r}
forms
a Cocteau category.

One can also extract an example of a Cocteau category of coherence spaces as they are described in
[
Mel09
, Section 8.10]
.

The notion of a
\Sigma
-assemblage is a direct generalisation of Cocteau categories for an arbitrary subexponential signature
\Sigma
.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
\Sigma=(I,\preceq,W,C)
be a subexponential signature,
a
\Sigma
-assemblage
4
4

4

The term is inspired by the notion of an assemblage from the philosophy of Gilles Deleuze and Felix Guattari.
is an SMCC
\mathcal{C}
(denoted as
(\mathcal{C},{\Sigma})
) equipped with a family of comonads

\{\bang_{s}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\>|\>s\in I\}
such that:

•

The mapping
s\mapsto\bang_{s}
is a contravariant functor from
(I,\preceq)
to the (1-)category of comonads over
\mathcal{C}
, so
if
s_{1}\preceq s_{2}
, then there is a comonad morphism
\mu(s_{1},s_{2}):\bang_{s_{2}}\Rightarrow\bang_{s_{1}}
,

•

If
s\in W
(
s\in C
), then
\bang_{s}
is an affine (relevant) comonad,

•

If
s\in W\cap C
, then
\bang_{s}
is an exponential comonad,

•

If
s_{1}\in C
and
s_{1}\preceq s_{2}
, then for each
A\in\mathcal{C}
,
\mu(s_{1},s_{2})_{A}:\bang_{s_{2}}A\to\bang_{s_{1}}A

induces a cocommutative cosemigroup morphism.

•

If both
s_{1},s_{2}\in W\cap C
and
s_{1}\preceq s_{2}
, then
\mu(s_{1},s_{2})_{A}:(\bang_{s_{2}}A,{\mathsf{c}_{s_{2}}}_{A},{\mathsf{d}_{s_{2}}}_{A})\to(\bang_{s_{1}}A,{\mathsf{c}_{s_{1}}}_{A},{\mathsf{d}_{s_{1}}}_{A})

induces a cocommutative comonoid morphism for each
A\in\mathcal{C}
.

•

If
s_{1}\in W
and
s_{1}\preceq s_{2}
, then the following triangle commutes for each
A\in\mathcal{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s_{2}}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s_{1}}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu(s_{1},s_{2})}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{d}_{s_{2}}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{d}_{s_{1}}}_{A}}

4.2
On Soundness and Completeness

Now we formulate the theorem of semantic adequacy of
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}

with respect to
\Sigma
-assemblages for a subexponential signature
\Sigma
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},{\Sigma})
be a
\Sigma
-assemblage over an SMCC
\mathcal{C}
and let

\mathcal{I}
be a function mapping each atomic to some object of
\mathcal{C}
.
The Cocteau interpretation function
[\![.]\!]
assigns an object of
\mathcal{C}
to a type as follows.

•

[\![p_{i}]\!]=\mathcal{I}(p_{i})
,

•

[\![\mathds{1}]\!]=\mathds{1}
,

•

[\![A\otimes B]\!]=[\![A]\!]\otimes[\![B]\!]
,

•

[\![A\multimap B]\!]=[[\![A]\!],[\![B]\!]]
,

•

[\![\bang_{s}A]\!]=\bang_{s}[\![A]\!]
,

Construction 4.7
.

Given a
\Sigma
-assemblage
(\mathcal{C},{\Sigma})
,
the interpretation of
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
-promotion rule is given as follows for
s\preceq s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
:

\Gamma_{1}\xrightarrow{M_{1}}\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n}\xrightarrow{M_{n}}\bang_{s_{n}}A_{n}

\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\bang_{s_{n}}A_{n}\xrightarrow{N}\psi

\otimes_{k}\Gamma_{k}\xrightarrow{\bang_{s}(N)\circ\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s}A_{1},\ldots,\bang_{s}A_{n}}\circ\otimes_{k}\mu({s,s_{k}})_{\bang_{s_{k}}A_{k}}\circ\otimes_{k}\delta^{s_{k}}_{A_{k}}\circ\otimes_{k}M_{k}}\bang A

Let us visualise the above derivation with a diagram for
n=2
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1}\otimes\Gamma_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}(\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang^{2}_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang^{2}_{s_{2}}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s}\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\otimes M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}}

The interpretation of the rest of the inference rules are given in Figure

. The only difference with the syntactic

Theorem 4.8
.

Every
\Sigma
-assemblage is a model of
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
and its
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
-equalities in context.
Moreover, there exists a
\Sigma
-assemblage
\mathcal{C}_{\Sigma}
with the following property:
if
\Gamma\vdash M:A
and
\Gamma\vdash N:A
such that
[\![M]\!]=[\![N]\!]
, then
the equality in context
\Gamma\vdash M\equiv N:A
is probable in
the typed equational logic generated the proof conversion rules from
Figures
3
,
4
,
5
,
6

and
7
.

The only difference with the proof-theoretic presentation is that we give the exchange rule explicitly.

Proof.

First of all, we observe that any Cocteau interpretation respects substitutions.
That is,
let
\Gamma\vdash M:A
and
\Delta_{1},x:A,\Delta_{2}\vdash N:B
,
then

[\![\Delta,\Gamma\vdash N[x:=M]:B]\!]=[\![N]\!]\circ 1_{[\![\Delta_{1}]\!]}\otimes[\![M]\!]\otimes 1_{[\![\Delta_{2}]\!]}.

The proof is rather standard and analogous to the proof
[
AT11
, Proposition 99]
.

Induction on the generation of
\Gamma\vdash M\equiv N:A
. Let us consider one of the cases involving comonad morphisms.
Consider the
({\bf C}_{{\operatorname{conv}_{1}}})
-rule. Let
s\in C
and
s\preceq s_{1},s_{2}
.
Assume we have the following contraction for:

x_{1}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\vdash x_{1}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}

y:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},z:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},x_{2}:\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}\vdash N:A

x_{1}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},x_{2}:\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}\vdash{\bf let}_{s_{1}}y,z=x_{1}\>{\bf in}\>N:A

The interpretation is given below:

\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes 1_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}}\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}\xrightarrow{N}A

And then we apply the promotion rule for
\Gamma_{1}\vdash M_{1}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}
and

\Gamma_{2}\vdash M_{2}:\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}
being derivable somehow:

\Gamma_{1}\vdash M_{1}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\Gamma_{2}\vdash M_{2}:\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}

x_{1}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},x_{2}:\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}\vdash{\bf let}_{s_{1}}y,z=x_{1}\>{\bf in}\>N:A

\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\vdash\bang_{s}({\bf let}_{s_{1}}y,z=x_{1}\>{\bf in}\>N)\>{\bf with}\>x_{1},x_{2}=M_{1},M_{2}:\bang_{s}A

Here is the interpretation of the above derivation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1}\otimes\Gamma_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}(\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang^{2}_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang^{2}_{s_{2}}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s}\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\otimes M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}N\circ\bang_{s}(\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes 1_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}}

On the other hand, we could make the following promotion first:

y^{\prime}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\vdash y^{\prime}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},z^{\prime}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\vdash z^{\prime}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\Gamma_{2}\vdash M_{2}:\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}

y:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},z:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},x_{2}:\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}\vdash N:A

y^{\prime}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},z^{\prime}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\Gamma_{2}\vdash\bang_{s}N\>{\bf with}\>y,z,x_{2}=y^{\prime},z^{\prime},M_{2}:\bang_{s}A

And then we apply the contraction rule

\Gamma_{1}\vdash M_{1}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}

y^{\prime}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},z^{\prime}:\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\Gamma_{2}\vdash\bang_{s}N\>{\bf with}\>y,z,x_{2}=y^{\prime},z^{\prime},M_{2}:\bang_{s}A

\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\vdash{\bf let}_{s_{1}}\>y^{\prime},z^{\prime}=M_{1}\>{\bf in}\>(\bang_{s}N\>{\bf with}\>y,z,x_{2}=y^{\prime},z^{\prime},M_{2}):\bang_{s}A

and obtain the following interpretation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1}\otimes\Gamma_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\Gamma_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\Gamma_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}(\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang^{2}_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang^{2}_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang^{2}_{s_{2}}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s}\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s}\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\otimes 1_{\Gamma_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}\otimes 1_{\Gamma_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{s}N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}}

Then one has:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\bang_{s}N\circ\bang_{s}(\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes 1_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}})\circ\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}}\circ M_{1}\otimes M_{2}=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{The definition of a lax monoidal functor}&\\
&\bang_{s}N\circ\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\bang_{s}(\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}})\otimes\bang_{s}(1_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}})\circ\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}}\circ M_{1}\otimes M_{2}&\\
\end{array}

Now we fix the composition
\bang_{s}N\circ\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}

and proceed with the rest of the big composite without
M_{1}\otimes M_{2}
.

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\bang_{s}(\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}})\otimes\bang_{s}(1_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}})\circ\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}}=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{The functoriality of $\otimes$}&\\
&((\bang_{s}(\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}})\circ\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}})\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}})\circ\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}}=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{The naturality of $\mu$}&\\
&((\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\circ\bang_{s_{1}}(\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}))\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}})\circ\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}}=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{The functoriality of $\otimes$}&\\
&(\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\circ\bang_{s_{1}}(\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}})\circ\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}})\otimes(\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}})=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{The definition of a relevant comonad}&\\
&(\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\circ\mathsf{m}^{s_{1}}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\circ\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\circ\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}})\otimes(\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}})=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{$\mu$ is a lax monoidal natural transformation}&\\
&(\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\circ\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\circ\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\circ\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}})\otimes(\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}})=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{Identity}&\\
&(\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\circ\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\circ\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\circ\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}})\otimes(\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}}\circ 1_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}})=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{The functoriality of $\otimes$}&\\
&(\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\circ\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}})\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes\delta^{s_{2}}_{A_{2}}\circ\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes 1_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}&\\
\end{array}

Next, we observe that the following holds

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ(\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\circ\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}})\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{The functoriality of $\otimes$}&\\
&\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}\otimes\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes 1_{\bang_{s_{2}}\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{The definition of a lax monoidal functor}&\\
&\mathsf{m}^{s}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1},\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{1})}_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes\mu{(s,s_{2})}_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}&\\
\end{array}

Let us take
\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes 1_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}
and precompose it with
M_{1}\otimes M_{2}
. And then we observe that:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\otimes 1_{\bang_{s_{2}}A_{2}}\circ M_{1}\otimes M_{2}=(\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\circ M_{1})\otimes M_{2}=&\\
&\>\>\>\>(1_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes 1_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\circ\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\circ M_{1})\otimes(M_{2}\circ 1_{\Gamma_{2}})=(1_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes 1_{\bang_{s_{1}}A_{1}}\otimes M_{2})\circ(\mathsf{c}^{s_{1}}_{A_{1}}\circ M_{1}\otimes 1_{\Gamma_{2}})&\\
\end{array}

The completeness part is proved by construction the free
\Sigma
-assemblage from the typing rules and
proof conversions from
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
similarly to
[
AT11
, §1.1.6]
.
∎

5
Characterising Relevant Categories

This is an auxillary section where we discuss some properties of relevant categories, that is,
symmetric monoidal categories where every object can be copied. The name “relevant category” comes from
that such categories are models for substructural logics admitting the contaction rule, that is, relevant logics.
We will not be going into the categorical analysis of relevant logic and related substructural logics,
but we refer the reader to
[
Pet02
]
and
[
Jac94
]
.

Definition 5.1
.

A symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
admits
duplication
(or
copying
) if
there is a symmetric monoidal natural transformation
\mathsf{copy}:1_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow 1_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{C}}

such that the following is satisfied:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes(A\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{copy}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{copy}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}\otimes\mathsf{copy}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{A,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A,A,A}\circ\mathsf{copy}_{A}\otimes 1_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{copy}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{copy}_{A}}

We also call such categories
relevant
categories.

The following fact about relevant categories is a useful tautology:

Proposition 5.2
.

An SMC
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1})
is relevant iff there is a symmetric monoidal natural transformation
with the components
\gamma_{A}:A\to A\otimes A
such that there is a cocommutative cosemigroup
(A,\gamma_{A})

for each
A\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
.

One can think of the above fact as the counterpart of Fox’s theorem
[
Fox76
]
for cocommutative cosemigroups.
Moreover, there is a more non-trivial equivalent characterisation of relevant categories that one can think
a version of the Eckmann-Hilton argument
[
EH62
]
for cocommutative cosemigroups.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1})
be a symmetric monoidal category.

1.

The category
{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})
of cocommutative cosemigroups is relevant.

2.

The endofunctor
{\bf coSem}:{\bf SymmMonCat}_{\operatorname{str}}\to{\bf SymmMonCat}_{\operatorname{str}}
forms
a comonad over
{\bf SymmMonCat}_{\operatorname{str}}
, a category of symmetric monoidal categories and
strict
symmetric monoidal functors.

3.

\mathcal{C}
is relevant iff the forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}_{\mathcal{C}}:{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
has a strict symmetric monoidal section

\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\to{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

1.

We define the duplication operation
(A,\gamma_{A})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{copy}_{(A,\gamma_{A})}}(A\otimes A,\gamma_{A\otimes A})

by
\gamma_{A}:A\to A\otimes A
. Then
\gamma_{A\otimes A}=\gamma_{A}\otimes\gamma_{A}
,
which is proved similarly to
[
Mel09
, Corollary 17]
for cocommutative comonoids.
Further, let us show that
\mathsf{copy}_{(A,\gamma_{A})}:(A,\gamma_{A})\to(A\otimes A,\gamma_{A\otimes A})
is natural in
(A,\gamma_{A})
.
Indeed, if
f:(A,\gamma_{A})\to(B,\gamma_{B})
is a cocommutative cosemigroup morphism, then the below diagram in
{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})

:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A,\gamma_{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B,\gamma_{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes A,\gamma_{A\otimes A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes B,\gamma_{B\otimes B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{copy}_{(A,\gamma_{A})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{copy}_{(B,\gamma_{B})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}

translates to the following diagram in
\mathcal{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes A)\otimes(A\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes B)\otimes(B\otimes B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}\otimes\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{B}\otimes\gamma_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\otimes f)\otimes(f\otimes f)}

The top diagram commutes by the definition of a cosemigroup morphism, the bottom diagram also commutes
by the definition of a cosemigroup morphism combined with the functoriality of tensor product.
The axioms of a relevant category follow directly from the definition of a cocommutative cosemigroup.

2.

The forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}_{\mathcal{C}}
is obviously strict and symmetric, so let us check that

{\bf coSem}
is a comonad. The components of the counit
\varepsilon:{\bf coSem}\Rightarrow 1
are given by
the forgetful functors
\mathsf{U}_{\mathcal{C}}:{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}

for each symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
. The naturality of
\varepsilon
is immediate.

The components of the comultiplication are defined the following way.
Let us specify the components of comultiplication

\delta_{\mathcal{C}}:{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})\to{\bf coSem}({\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C}))

for an SMC
\mathcal{C}
as follows. Let
(A,\gamma_{A})\in{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})
be a cocommutative cosemigroup
in
\mathcal{C}
, then we put
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}:(A,\gamma_{A})\mapsto((A,\gamma_{A}),\gamma_{(A,\gamma_{A})})
, where

\gamma_{(A,\gamma_{A})}:(A,\gamma_{A})\to(A,\gamma_{A})\otimes(A,\gamma_{A})=(A\otimes A,\gamma_{A\otimes A})

where the right-hand side is a cocommutative cosemigroup from the previous part of this lemma. Note that

(A,\gamma_{A})
should be necessarily cocommutative so we can conclude that
(A,\gamma_{A})\otimes(A,\gamma_{A})

exists by Theorem
3.19
.
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}
is defined on morphisms similarly.
The rest is check naturality for
\delta
,
that is, the following square commutes for SMC’s
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
and a strict symmetric monoidal functor

\mathsf{F}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}^{2}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}^{2}(\mathcal{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\mathsf{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}^{2}(\mathsf{F})}

which is routine.

Let us check the axioms of comonad. That is, the claim is that the following squares commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}^{2}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}^{2}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}^{2}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}^{3}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}^{2}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{{\bf coSem}\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\delta_{\mathcal{C}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\varepsilon_{\mathcal{C}})}

Indeed, let
\mathsf{A}=(A,\gamma_{A})
and
\mathsf{A}=(B,\gamma_{B})
be cocommutative cosemigroups in
\mathcal{C}
, then
let us show that left-hand side square for cocommutative cosemigroups. The conditions for morphisms are checked similarly.

On the one hand, we have:

\delta_{{\bf coSem}\mathcal{C}}(\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\gamma_{A}))=\delta_{{\bf coSem}\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{A},\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{A}})=((\mathsf{A},\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{A}}),\mathsf{copy}_{(\mathsf{A},\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{A}})})=({\bf coSem}(\delta_{\mathcal{C}}))(\mathsf{A},\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{A}})

The right-hand side square in a similarly:

\varepsilon_{{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}(\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\gamma_{A}))=\varepsilon_{{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}(\mathsf{A},\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{A}})=(A,\gamma_{A})={\bf coSem}(\varepsilon_{\mathcal{C}})(\mathsf{A},\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{A}}).

3.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be relevant, then there is a natural transformation

\mathsf{copy}:1_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow 1_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{C}}
such that its component each
A\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})

is endowed with the structure of a cocommutative cosemigroup with the comultiplication operation given by
the corresponding component.
\mathsf{copy}_{A}:A\to A\otimes A
.
Put
\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}:A\mapsto(A,\mathsf{copy}_{A})
. Then it is readily checked that
\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a required strict monoidal section.

Now assume that the forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}_{\mathcal{C}}:{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}

has a strict section
\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\to{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})
. In the previous part of the lemma, we showed that
{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})
is relevant,
so for each
A\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
there is an arrow

\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}A}:\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}A\to\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}A\otimes\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}A
in
{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})
and, therefore,
one has
\mathsf{U}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}A}):A\to A\otimes A
in
\mathcal{C}
,
so we let
\gamma_{A}:=\mathsf{U}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}A})
.

∎

We can also characterise relevant categories as coalgebras in the Eilenberg-Moore category

{\bf SymmMonCat}_{\operatorname{str}}^{\bf coSem}
consisting of coalgebras

(\mathcal{C},h_{\mathcal{C}})
where
h_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\to{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})

is a strict symmetric monoidal functor.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an SMC, then the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{C}
is relevant,

2.

(\mathcal{C},h_{\mathcal{C}})\in{\bf SymmMonCat}_{\operatorname{str}}^{\bf coSem}
for
some strict symmetric monoidal functor
h_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\to{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{C}
is relevant, then the forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}_{\mathcal{C}}:{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}

has a strict section
\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\to{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})
by Lemma
5.3
. Let us check that a pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathsf{V})

is the required
{\bf coSem}
-coalgebra, that is, the below diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}^{2}(\mathcal{C})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{U}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}})}

The left-hand side triangle commutes automatically since
\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a strict symmetric section of the
forgetful functor. The right-hand side square commutes since if
\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}}(A)=(A,\gamma_{A})
, then
{\bf coSem}(\mathsf{V}_{\mathcal{C}})(A,\gamma_{A})=(A\otimes A,\gamma_{A\otimes A})=\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\gamma_{A})
.
But if
(\mathcal{C},h_{\mathcal{C}})
is already a coalgebra in the category
{\bf SymmMonCat}_{\operatorname{str}}^{\bf coSem}
,
then
h_{\mathcal{C}}
is already a section of the forgetful functor by the definition of an Eilenberg-Moore category.
∎

6
Cocteau Categories and Resource Modalities

In this section, we discuss how one can view comonads from Cocteau categories
as monoidal adjunctions. To be more precise, we would like to discuss whether such
comonads can be materialised as resource modalities, the term coined by Melliès and Tabareau in
[
MT10
]

to categorise affine and relevant subexponential modalities in the fashion
of Benton’s linear-non-linear models
[
Ben94
]
. There is a well-known result in categorical semantics
of linear logic allowing one to unveil
\bang
as a symmetric monoidal adjunction (see
[
Ben94
]
),
so in this section we extend this result to
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{3}
and
{\bf SILL}(\lambda)_{\Sigma}
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal (closed) category, then a
modality
is given by a symmetric monoidal adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

for a symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{D}
.
The notation is
((\mathsf{F}_{l},\mathsf{n}),(\mathsf{F}_{r},\mathsf{o}),\mathcal{D}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
,
where
\mathsf{n}
and
\mathsf{o}
are components of
\mathsf{F}_{l}
and
\mathsf{F}_{r}
respectively

5
5

5

Sometimes we would just write
(\mathsf{F}_{l},\mathsf{F}_{r},\mathcal{D}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
assuming that
the letters
\mathsf{n}
and
\mathsf{o}
are already reserved for the components of a left and a right adjoint respectively.
.

The key observation is that the composite
\mathsf{F}_{l}\circ\mathsf{F}_{r}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
is a symmetric lax monoidal comonad
and we shall refer to such composites as modalities on an SMC
\mathcal{C}
.
Although the notion of a modality coincides with the notion of a monoidal adjunction,
we proceed with the term “modality” inspired by Yetter
[
Yet90
]
, who used this name for a quantic conucleus
over a (commutative) quantale, whose formal properties coincide with the properties of (symmetric) lax monoidal comonads.
For comparison, Melliès and Tabareau
[
MT07
,
MT10
]
call such adjunctions
“resource modalities”, but further we will consider a broader class of comonadic modal operators, so we reserve the term
“resource modality” only for the modalities dealing with resource management policies.

As Benton showed in
[
Ben94
, Theorem 3, Theorem 8]
, every
linear category induces a symmetric monoidal adjunction between an SMCC and a Cartesian closed category.
Such adjunctions are called
linear-non-linear models
, and they are an instance of the concept of a modality.
However, as it is was discussed in
[
Mel09
]
, one can weaken the definition of a linear-non-linear model and construct
the desired adjunction between an SMCC and a Cartesian category, not necessarily admitting exponential objects.
We are in favour the latter way since it is less restrictive.

In this section we will show that every Cocteau category can be also viewed as an SMCC equipped
with symmetric monoidal adjunctions of a particular kind.

In the previous section, we have already done some preliminary work on how one can characterise relevant categories
to use those results further to represent relevant subexponentials as a monoidal adjunction. Let us discuss
what monoidal categories we need to obtain further a similar characterisation for affine subexponentials.

Definition 6.2
.

A symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
is
semicartesian

where the unit object is terminal. That is, there is a unique
\top_{A}:A\to\mathds{1}
for each
A\in\mathcal{C}
.

Note that the tensor product operation admits projections in every semicartesian category:

\pi_{1}:A\otimes B\xrightarrow{1_{A}\otimes\top_{B}}A\otimes\mathds{1}\xrightarrow{\lambda_{A}}A

\pi_{2}:A\otimes B\xrightarrow{\top_{A}\otimes 1_{B}}\mathds{1}\otimes B\xrightarrow{\rho_{B}}B

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1})
be any monoidal category, then the slice category
\mathcal{C}/\mathds{1}
becomes semicartesian with the tensor product
operation given by for
f,g\in\mathcal{C}/\mathds{1}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda=\rho}

\mathcal{C}/\mathds{1}
is semicartesian since the monoidal identity
1_{\mathds{1}}:\mathds{1}\to\mathds{1}
is terminal
in
\mathcal{C}/\mathds{1}
.
One can also observe that a symmetric monoidal category is semicartesian iff the forgetful functor

\mathsf{U}:\mathcal{C}/\mathds{1}\to\mathcal{C}
is isomorphism.

Now we give the definition of a resource modality.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an SMCC and let
\mathcal{D}
be an SMC, then a modality
(\mathsf{F}_{l},\mathsf{F}_{r},\mathcal{D}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
is

affine
if
\mathcal{D}
is semicartesian,

relevant
if
\mathcal{D}
is relevant,
exponential
if
\mathcal{D}
is Cartesian.

A modality satisfying any of the above conditions is called a
resource modality
.

Let us modify some well-known results in the categorical semantics of linear logic and show that
every Cocteau category can be unveiled as a category equipped with resource modality and vice versa and
then transfer this result to
\Sigma
-assemblages.

Definition 6.4
.

A
Cocteau adjunction
on an SMCC
\mathcal{C}
is given by:

•

an affine resource modality
((\mathsf{A}_{l},\mathsf{n}^{\bf a}),(\mathsf{A}_{r},\mathsf{o}^{\bf a}),\mathcal{C}_{\bf a}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
,

•

a relevant resource modality
((\mathsf{R}_{l},\mathsf{n}^{\bf r}),(\mathsf{R}_{r},\mathsf{o}^{\bf r}),\mathcal{C}_{\bf r}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
,

•

an exponential resource modality
((\mathsf{I}_{l},\mathsf{n}^{\bf i}),(\mathsf{I}_{r},\mathsf{o}^{\bf i}),\mathcal{C}_{\bf i}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
,

•

strict symmetric monoidal functors
\mathsf{a}:\mathcal{C}_{\bf i}\to\mathcal{C}_{\bf a}
and
\mathsf{r}:\mathcal{C}_{\bf i}\to\mathcal{C}_{\bf r}

such that the pairs
(\mathsf{a},1_{\mathcal{C}})
and
(\mathsf{r},1_{\mathcal{C}})
are left adjunction morphisms

(\mathsf{A}_{l},\mathsf{n}^{\bf a})\dashv(\mathsf{A}_{r},\mathsf{o}^{\bf a})
and
(\mathsf{R}_{l},\mathsf{n}^{\bf r})\dashv(\mathsf{A}_{r},\mathsf{o}^{\bf r})

respectively, that is, the following diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\bf i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\bf a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\bf i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\bf r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{I}_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{A}_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{I}_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}}

and the following axioms are satisfied for
A\in\mathcal{C}_{\bf i}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{r}(A\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{a}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{a}\mathsf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{r}A\otimes\mathsf{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{r}(\Delta_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{r}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{a}(\top_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top_{\mathsf{a}A}}

6.1
Modalities from Adjunctions

This is a folklore fact from basic category theory that the composition of adjoint functors is a comonad.
However, the concept of a Cocteau adjunction also has strict functors to reflect the corresponding comonad morphism.
Therefore, we need a slightly more complicated proof compared to a standard argument in linear logic
showing how one can obtain a linear category from a linear-non-linear model as, for example, in

[
Mel09
, §7.4]
.

Theorem 6.5
.

Every Cocteau adjunction is a Cocteau category.

Proof.

Let
(\mathsf{R}_{l},\mathsf{R}_{r},\mathcal{C}_{\bf r}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

be a relevant resource modality on
\mathcal{C}
, let us show that the composite
\bang_{\bf r}=\mathsf{R}_{l}\circ\mathsf{R}_{r}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

forms a relevant comonad on
\mathcal{C}
. The components are given as follows:

•

\mathsf{m}^{\bf r}_{A,B}:=\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}B\xrightarrow{\mathsf{n}^{\bf r}_{\mathsf{R}_{r}A,\mathsf{R}_{r}B}}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{r}B)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{R}_{l}(\mathsf{o}^{\bf r}_{A,B})}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}(A\otimes B)
,

•

\mathsf{m}^{\bf r}_{\mathds{1}}:=\mathds{1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{n}^{\bf r}_{\mathds{1}}}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathds{1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{R}_{l}(\mathsf{o}^{\bf r}_{\mathds{1}})}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathds{1}
,

•

\varepsilon^{\bf r}_{A}:\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\to A
,

•

\delta^{\bf r}_{A}:\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\xrightarrow{\mathsf{R}_{l}(\eta^{\bf r}_{\mathsf{R}_{r}A})}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A
.

A similar statement holds for affine and exponential resource modalities. The copying natural transformation

\mathsf{c}
is given by the components
\mathsf{c}_{A}:\bang_{\bf r}A\to\bang_{\bf r}A\otimes\bang_{\bf r}A
, each of which is defined by the following composite:

\bang_{\bf r}A=\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\xrightarrow{\mathsf{R}_{l}(\mathsf{copy}_{{\mathsf{R}_{r}A}})}\mathsf{R}_{l}(\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{r}A)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{p}_{\mathsf{R}_{r}A,\mathsf{R}_{r}B}}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A=\bang_{\bf r}A\otimes\bang_{\bf r}A.

There are the following claims, the complete checking of which is routine.

1.

\mathsf{c}_{A}
is natural in
A
, so for any
f:A\to B
the following squares commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}B\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}f\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}f}

2.

\mathsf{c}
is a monoidal natural transformation, so the diagrams from Definition
3.21
are satisfied.

3.

For each
A\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
, the axiom of comonoidal comultiplication are satisfied:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes(\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}{\bf r}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}\otimes\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\bang_{\bf r}A,\bang_{\bf r}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A,A,A}\circ(\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}_{A}\otimes 1_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}_{A}}

But they follow from the structure of
\mathcal{C}_{\bf r}
easily.

4.

Any free coalgebra morphism
f:(\bang_{\bf r}A,\delta_{A})\to(\bang_{\bf r}B,\delta_{B})
preserves comultiplication induced by
\mathsf{c}
, that is, the
following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}A\otimes\bang_{\bf r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}B\otimes\bang_{\bf r}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}

One can similarly show that
\bang_{\bf a}
is endowed with the structure of an affine comonad, where the deletion is given by the composite

\bang_{\bf a}A\xrightarrow{\mathsf{A}_{l}(\top_{\mathsf{A}^{*}A})}\mathsf{A}_{l}\mathds{1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{p}_{\mathds{1}}}\mathds{1}
.

To complete the proof, we need to construct the comonad morphisms
\mu_{\bf ia}:\bang_{\bf i}\Rightarrow\bang_{\bf a}
and
\mu_{\bf ir}:\bang_{\bf i}\Rightarrow\bang_{\bf r}

from the functors
\mathsf{a}:\mathcal{C}_{\bf i}\to\mathcal{C}_{\bf a}
and
\mathsf{r}:\mathcal{C}_{\bf i}\to\mathcal{C}_{\bf r}
.
The symmetric lax monoidal comonad morphism
\mu_{\bf ir}:\mathsf{I}_{l}\mathsf{I}_{r}\Rightarrow\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}

is given by Proposition
3.22
since we have
\mathsf{I}_{l}\mathsf{I}_{r}=\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}

by the definition of a Cocteau adjunction.

So we let:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{I}_{l}\mathsf{I}_{r}=\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}=\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathsf{I}_{l}\mathsf{I}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\bf ir}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}(\eta^{\bf r}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}\varepsilon^{\bf i}}

Further, we note that a pair of functors
(\mathsf{r},1_{\mathcal{C}})
, then by Proposition
3.9
,

\mu_{\bf ir}
is a symmetric lax monoidal comonad morphism. The rest is to check that
\mu_{\bf ir}
preserves relevant comultiplication, that is, we must show that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf i}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf i}A\otimes\bang_{\bf i}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}A\otimes\bang_{\bf r}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf i}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\otimes{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}_{A}}

(5)

Let us unveil the above diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}(\mathsf{I}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{I}_{r}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}(\mathsf{I}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{I}_{r}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}(\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}(\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{r}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}(\Delta_{\mathsf{I}_{r}A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}^{\bf i}_{\mathsf{I}_{r}A,\mathsf{I}_{r}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}(\eta^{\bf r}_{\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A})\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}(\eta^{\bf r}_{\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}(\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}(\eta^{\bf r}_{\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}(\eta^{\bf r}_{\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A}\otimes\eta^{\bf r}_{\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}(\varepsilon^{\bf i}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}(\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}(\mathsf{R}_{r}(\varepsilon^{\bf i}_{A})\otimes\mathsf{R}_{r}(\varepsilon^{\bf i}_{A}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}^{\bf r}_{\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A,\mathsf{R}_{r}\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}\mathsf{I}_{r}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}(\varepsilon^{\bf i}_{A})\otimes\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{R}_{r}(\varepsilon^{\bf i}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{R}_{l}(\mathsf{copy}_{\mathsf{R}_{r}A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}^{\bf r}_{\mathsf{R}_{r}A,\mathsf{R}_{r}A}}
\make@display@tag
\make@display@tag
\make@display@tag
\make@display@tag
\make@display@tag

where:

•

(
6.1
) holds by the definition of a Cocteau adjunction,

•

(
6.1
) and (
6.1
) hold by the naturality of
\mathsf{copy}
,

•

(
6.1
) and (
6.1
) commute since
\mathsf{R}_{l}
is symmetric oplax monoidal by Proposition
3.22
.

∎

6.2
Constructing a Cocteau adjunction from a Cocteau category

To show that every Cocteau category induces a Cocteau adjunction, one needs to show that
every constituent comonad of a Cocteau category induces the corresponding adjunction.
The construction building an adjunction from an exponential comonad is standard, so we just note
that the construction by Benton or Melliès is compeletely preserved since one can think of linear categories
as exponential reducts of Cocteau categories. So the rest is build adjunctions from affine and relevant comonads and introduce the
corresponding functors to have required comonad morphism.

The simplest part is to unveil an affine comonad.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an SMCC equipped with an affine comonad
\mathsf{K}
, then there is
an affine resource modality:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\bf a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{A}_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{A}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

for some semicartesian
\mathcal{C}_{\bf a}
,
\mathsf{A}_{l}
and
\mathsf{A}_{r}
.

Proof.

The Eilenberg-Moore category
\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{K}}
is already monoidal by Lemma
3.24
.
We equip every coalgebra
(A,h_{A})
with the deletion operation
\top_{A}:A\to\mathds{1}
natural in
A
and unique for each

A
such that
\top_{A}:A\to\mathds{1}
is a coalgebra morphism and
\top_{A}
is given by:

A\xrightarrow{\iota_{A}}\mathds{1}=A\xrightarrow{h_{A}}\mathsf{K}A\xrightarrow{\mathsf{d}_{A}}\mathds{1}

The rest of the proof is routine.
∎

The next is to show that the Eilenberg-Moore category of a relevant comonad is relevant.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an SMC and let
\mathsf{K}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

be a symmetric lax monoidal endofunctor endowed with the structure of a relevant comonad.
Then the Eilenberg-Moore category
\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{K}}
is relevant.

The proof is adaptation of the construction described in
[
Mel09
, §7.4]
with a few modifications,
but the structure of the argument is similar. The key idea is that if we have a relevant comonad, then every free coalgebra

(\mathsf{K}A,\delta_{A})
is endowed with a cocommutative cosemigroup structure and we lift this structure
to every coalgebra
(A,h_{A})
by showing that every coalgebra of that form is retract of the free coalgebra

(\mathsf{K}A,\delta_{A})
. As a result, every coalgebra
(A,h_{A})
is provided with a cosemigroup comultiplication

\gamma_{A}:A\to A\otimes A
satisfying the required axioms. One can compose
the proof of Lemma
6.7
of the following two propositions.

Proposition 6.8
.

Every coalgebra
(A,h_{A})
has a retraction
A\xrightarrow{h_{A}}\mathsf{K}A\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{A}}A

making the below diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\varepsilon_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}\otimes h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}_{A}}

Proof.

The below claim has the same proof as
[
Mel09
, §7.4, Proposition 26]
for comonoids
since the argument does not require addressing to counits, but only comultiplication.
∎

The below claim is similar to
[
Mel09
, §7.4, Proposition 27]
, but, however, we elaborate on its proof.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
and let
(B,\gamma_{B})
be a cocommutative cosemigroup in
\mathcal{C}
such that
there is a retraction in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Then
A
is endowed with the structure of a cocommutative cosemigroup
(A,\gamma_{A})
that lifts
f
to a cosemigroup
morphism
f:(A,\gamma_{A})\to(B,\gamma)
if and only if the below diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\otimes g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{B}}

Moreover, the cosemigroup structure on
A
is defined where the comultiplication operation

\gamma_{A}
is given by:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\otimes g}

Proof.

If
A
is endowed with the structure of a cocommutative cosemigroup
(A,\gamma_{A})

such that a morphism
f:A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
lifts to
a cosemigroup morphism
f:(A,\gamma_{A})\to(B,\gamma_{A})
, so the below square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}

But the following square commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\otimes g}

since one has
g\circ f=1_{A}
. So we define
\gamma_{A}
as the
composite
g\otimes g\circ\gamma_{B}\circ f
, so the desired diagram automatically commutes.

If the diagram from the statement commutes, then one needs to check that the structure

(A,\gamma_{A})
where
\gamma_{A}=g\otimes g\circ\gamma_{B}\circ f
is a cocommutative cosemigroup indeed,
so the cocommutativity and coassociativity axioms are satisfied for the comultiplication operation.
The coassociativity clause is the same as in the proof of
[
Mel09
, §7.4, Proposition 27]
,
so let us consider the cocommutativity axiom. But
(B,\gamma_{B})
is already a cocommutative cosemigroup, so we have:

\sigma_{A,A}\circ g\otimes g\circ\gamma_{B}\circ f=g\otimes g\circ\sigma_{B,B}\circ\gamma_{B}\circ f=g\otimes g\circ\gamma_{B}\circ f=\gamma_{A}.

∎

As a corollary from Claim
6.8
and Claim
6.9
below we show that:

Lemma 6.10
.

Every coalgebra
(A,h_{A})
over a relevant comonad
\mathsf{K}
is endowed with a cocommutative cosemigroup multiplication making
the Eilenberg-Moore category
\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{K}}
relevant.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.2
, an SMC
\mathcal{C}
is relevant if and only if there is a natural
natural transformation
\gamma
endowing each
A
with the structure of a cocommutative cosemigroup
(A,\gamma_{A})
.

As we showed in Claim
6.8
and Claim
6.9
, we define
\gamma_{A}
as:

A\xrightarrow{h_{A}}\mathsf{K}A\xrightarrow{\mathsf{c}_{A}}\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}A\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\varepsilon_{A}}A\otimes A

The rest is to check the following clauses:

•

\gamma_{A}
is a coalgebra morphism, that is, we want the below square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{K}A\otimes\mathsf{K}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(A\otimes A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\varepsilon_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}\otimes h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}_{A,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(h_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{c}_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{K}(\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\varepsilon_{A})}

commute in
\mathcal{C}
. Indeed,

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\mathsf{K}(\gamma_{A})\circ h_{A}=\mathsf{K}(\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\varepsilon_{A})\circ\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{c}_{A})\circ\mathsf{K}(h_{A})\circ h_{A}=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{By the property of coalgebras}&\\
&\mathsf{K}(\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\varepsilon_{A})\circ\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{c}_{A})\circ\delta_{A}\circ h_{A}=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{By the definition of a relevant comonad and by the definiton of lax monoidal functors}&\\
&\mathsf{K}(\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\varepsilon_{A})\circ\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{K}A,\mathsf{K}A}\circ\delta_{A}\otimes\delta_{A}\circ\mathsf{c}_{A}\circ h_{A}=\mathsf{m}_{A,A}\circ(\mathsf{K}\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\mathsf{K}\varepsilon_{A})\circ\delta_{A}\otimes\delta_{A}\circ\mathsf{c}_{A}\circ h_{A}=&\\
&\>\>\>\>\text{By the definition of a comonad and Claim\penalty\ \ref{coalgebra:claim1}}&\\
&\mathsf{m}_{A,A}\circ\mathsf{c}_{A}\circ h_{A}=\mathsf{m}_{A,A}\circ h_{A}\otimes h_{A}\circ\gamma_{A}&\end{array}

•

The naturality of
\gamma_{A}
follows immediately from the definition of an Eilenberg-Moore category
and the axioms of a relevant comonad.

•

The proof that
\gamma_{A}
is a symmetric lax monoidal natural transformation
repeats the comultiplication clause in the proof of
[
Mel09
, §7.4, Proposition 28]
.

∎

Finally, we conclude that:

Theorem 6.11
.

Any Cocteau category induces a Cocteau adjunction.

Proof.

Fix a Cocteau category
(\mathcal{C},\bang_{\bf i},\bang_{\bf r},\bang_{\bf a},\mu_{\bf ir},\mu_{\bf ia})
.
We are already able to unveil
\bang_{\bf i}
,
\bang_{\bf r}
and
\bang_{\bf a}

as resource modalities of the following form

•

an affine resource modality
(\mathsf{A}_{l},\mathsf{A}_{r},\mathcal{C}_{\bf a}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
by Lemma
6.6
,

•

a relevant resource modality
(\mathsf{R}_{l},\mathsf{R}_{r},\mathcal{C}_{\bf r}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
by Lemma
6.7
,

•

an exponential resource modality
(\mathsf{I}_{l},\mathsf{I}_{r},\mathcal{C}_{\bf i})
by
[
Mel09
, Proposition 28—30]
.

The rest is to treat the comonad morphisms
\mu_{\bf ir}
and
\mu_{\bf ia}
. We consider
\mu_{\bf ir}
only,
\mu_{\bf ia}

is considered analogously.
\mu_{\bf ir}:\bang_{\bf i}\Rightarrow\bang_{\bf r}
is a comonad morphism, so,
by
[
Bor94b
, Proposition 4.5.9]
, we have a functor

\mathsf{r}:\mathcal{C}^{\bang_{\bf i}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\bang_{\bf r}}
such that

\mathsf{r}:(A,h_{A})\mapsto(A,{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\circ h_{A})
and
\mathsf{I}_{l}=\mathsf{R}_{l}\mathsf{r}
.

\mathsf{r}
is also strict:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\mathsf{r}(A,h_{A})\otimes\mathsf{r}(A,h_{A})=(A,{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\circ h_{A})\otimes(A,{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\circ h_{A})=(A\otimes A,\mathsf{m}^{\bf r}_{A,A}\circ{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\otimes{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\circ h_{A}\circ h_{A})=&\\
&\>\>\>\>(A\otimes A,{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\circ\mathsf{m}^{\bf i}_{A,A}\circ h_{A}\circ h_{A})=\mathsf{r}(A\otimes A,\mathsf{m}^{\bf i}_{A,A}\circ h_{A}\otimes h_{A})&\end{array}

And the corresponding axiom of a Cocteau adjunction is satisfies as follows. First of
all, let
\mathsf{copy}^{\bf i}
and
\mathsf{copy}^{\bf r}
denote the copying operations in
\mathcal{C}^{\bang_{\bf i}}

and
\mathcal{C}^{\bang_{\bf r}}
.

Let
(A,h_{A})\in\mathcal{C}^{\bang_{\bf i}}
be a coalgebra, so we have the comultiplication arrow

\mathsf{copy}^{\bf i}:(A,h_{A})\to(A\otimes A,\mathsf{m}_{A,A}\circ h_{A}\otimes h_{A})
realised by the composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf i}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf i}A\otimes\bang_{\bf i}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf i}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\bf i}_{A}\otimes\varepsilon^{\bf i}_{A}}

(6)

On the one hand, we have
\mathsf{r}(\mathsf{copy}^{\bf i}):\mathsf{r}(A,h_{A})\to\mathsf{r}(A\otimes A,\mathsf{m}_{A,A}\circ h_{A}\otimes h_{A})
with the underlying
arrow as in (
6
), but as a morphism of morphism of coalgebras

(A,{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\circ h_{A})
and
(A\otimes A,{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\circ\mathsf{m}^{\bf i}_{A,A}\circ h_{A}\circ h_{A})
.

On the other hand, we have the arrow
\mathsf{copy}^{\bf r}_{\mathsf{r}(A,h_{A})}:(A,{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\circ h_{A})\to(A\otimes A,{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A\otimes A}\circ\mathsf{m}^{\bf i}_{A,A}\circ h_{A}\otimes h_{A})
in
\mathcal{C}^{\bang_{\bf r}}

with the underlying arrow:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}A\otimes\bang_{\bf r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\bf r}_{A}\otimes\varepsilon^{\bf r}_{A}}

(7)

So the equality
\mathsf{r}(\mathsf{copy}^{\bf i}_{(A,h_{A})})=\mathsf{copy}^{\bf r}_{\mathsf{r}(A,h_{A})}
holds by the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf i}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf i}A\otimes\bang_{\bf i}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bang_{\bf r}A\otimes\bang_{\bf r}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf i}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\otimes{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\bf i}_{A}\otimes\varepsilon^{\bf i}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{\bf ir}}_{A}\circ h_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\bf r}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\bf r}_{A}\otimes\varepsilon^{\bf r}_{A}}

where the left-hand side square commutes tautologically, the right-hand side square follows from the definition of a
symmetric monoidal comonad morphism, whereas the central square is from the definition of a Cocteau category.
∎

6.3
Boutons for Characterising Assemblages

One can generalise the construction from the previous subsection in order to represent not only Cocteau categories,
but arbitrary assemblages as well. We introduce the notion of a bouton directly generalising Cocteau adjunctions.

Definition 6.12
.

Let
\Sigma=(I,\preceq,W,C)
be a subexponential signature and let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal closed category,
then a
\Sigma
-bouton
6
6

6

The word “bouton” means a flower bud in French. The choice of name is a metaphorical description of a category equipped with some adjunctions viewed as petals.
over
\mathcal{C}
consists of
a contravariant functor
\mathsf{T}:(I,\preceq)\to{\bf SymmMonCat}_{\operatorname{strict}}
such that:

•

For each
s\in I
, there is a modality
(\mathsf{F}^{s}_{l},\mathsf{F}^{s}_{r},\mathsf{T}_{s}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
,

•

If
s\in W
(
s\in C
,
c\in W\cap C
), then the resource modality

(\mathsf{F}^{s}_{l},\mathsf{F}^{s}_{r})
is affine (relevant, exponential),

•

If
s_{1}\preceq s_{2}
, then the following triangle commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{T}_{s_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{T}_{s_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}_{s_{2}}}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{T}{(s_{1},s_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}_{s_{1}}}_{r}}

•

If
s_{1}\preceq s_{2}
and
s_{1}\in C
, then the following triangle commutes for each
A\in\mathsf{T}_{s_{2}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{T}{(s_{1},s_{2})}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{T}{(s_{1},s_{2})}{(A\otimes A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{T}{(s_{1},s_{2})}(A)\otimes\mathsf{T}{(s_{1},s_{2})}(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{copy}^{s_{1}}_{\mathsf{T}{(s_{1},s_{2})}{(A)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{T}{(s_{1},s_{2})}{(\mathsf{copy}^{s_{2}}_{A})}}

•

If
s_{1}\preceq s_{2}
and
s_{1}\in W
, then the following triangle commutes for each
A\in\mathsf{T}_{s_{2}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{T}{(s_{1},s_{2})}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{T}{(s_{1},s_{2})}(\mathds{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top^{s_{1}}_{\mathsf{T}{(s_{1},s_{2})}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{T}{(s_{1},s_{2})}(\top^{s_{2}}_{A})}

The following fact directly generalises Theorem
6.5
and Theorem
6.11
.

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an SMCC and let
\Sigma=(I,\preceq,W,C)
be a subexponential signature, then:

1.

Every
\Sigma
-bouton on
\mathcal{C}
induces a
\Sigma
-assemblage.

2.

Every
\Sigma
-assemblage on
\mathcal{C}
induces a
\Sigma
-bouton.

6.4
Expanding Lafont Categories

The alternative approach to semantic modelling of the exponential modality is to consider it
via the free construction, see
[
Tro92
, Chapter 12]
.

Previously, we have been working with Cocteau categories thinking of them as an expansion of a linear category,
but in this section we introduce a subclass of Cocteau categories admitting the representation as Cocteau adjunctions.
In particular, we expand Lafont categories initially introduced in
[
Laf88
]
.

It is not difficult to observe that the axioms of a cocommutative cosemigroup resemble the axioms
of a relevant category quite closely, but this coincidence has the following rigorisation.

In the below definition, we overload the terminology and call them Lafont categories too.

Definition 6.14
.

A
Lafont
category is an SMCC
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1})
such that
the forgetful functors
\mathsf{U}_{\bf a}:\mathcal{C}/\mathds{1}\to\mathcal{C}
,

\mathsf{U}_{\bf i}:{\bf coMon}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
and

\mathsf{U}_{\bf r}:{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
have
right adjoints
\mathsf{R}_{\bf a}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}/\mathds{1}
,

\mathsf{R}_{\bf i}:\mathcal{C}\to{\bf coMon}(\mathcal{C})
and

\mathsf{R}_{\bf r}:\mathcal{C}\to{\bf coMon}(\mathcal{C})
respectively.
Those right adjoints are also called
the free construction
.

In every Lafont category,
\mathsf{U}_{\bf a}
,
\mathsf{U}_{\bf i}
and
\mathsf{U}_{\bf r}
are automatically
agreed with one another as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coMon}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coSem}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf coMon}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}/\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{U}_{\bf ir}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{U}_{\bf i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{U}_{\bf r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{U}_{\bf ia}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{U}_{\bf i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{U}_{\bf a}}

where
\mathsf{U}_{\bf ir}
and
\mathsf{U}_{\bf ia}
are strict monoidal functors realised as

\mathsf{U}_{\bf ir}:(A,\gamma_{A},\iota_{A})\mapsto(A,\gamma_{A})
and
\mathsf{U}_{\bf ia}:(A,\gamma_{A},\iota_{A})\mapsto(A,\iota_{A})

respectively.

One can equivalently define Lafont category as an SMCC
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathds{1})

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

•

For every
A\in\operatorname{Ob}({\mathcal{C}})
there is a cocommutative comonoid
(A_{\bf i},\gamma_{A_{\bf i}},\iota_{A_{\bf i}})

equipped with a choice of morphism
\varepsilon^{\bf i}_{A}:A_{\bf i}\to A
such that
